Family Court Rules 2002
Rules
1 Title
These rules are the Family Court Rules 2002.
 Part 1 — Preliminary provisions
 Commencement
2 Commencement
These rules come into force on 21 October 2002.
 Purpose
3 Purpose of these rules
The purpose of these rules is to make it possible for proceedings in the Family Court to be dealt with—
as fairly, inexpensively, simply, and speedily as is consistent with justice; and
in such a way as to avoid unnecessary formality; and
in harmony with the purpose and spirit of the family law Acts under which the proceedings arise.
These rules must be read in the light of their purpose.
 Overview
4 Overview of these rules
These rules are divided into 8 Parts and 11 schedules.
Part 1 contains preliminary provisions (for example, provisions about the purpose, general scheme and effect, and application of these rules).
Part 1A contains provisions about how the e-solution may or must be used to bring or deal with proceedings in the Family Court (see rule 5).
Part 2 sets out the general procedure in the Family Court.
Part 3 contains rules about particular aspects of proceedings in the Family Court (for example, service of documents).
Part 4 sets out procedures for dealing with interlocutory matters (that is, matters that arise in the course of proceedings).
Part 5 contains special rules for proceedings under certain family law Acts.
Part 5A sets out rules applying to proceedings under the Care of Children Act 2004 (other than proceedings under subpart 2 of Part 2 of that Act (which relates to international child abduction)).
Part 6 contains rules about Registry matters and transitional matters (for example, Family Court offices, Family Court office hours, records, access to documents and court files, the transfer of records or information, and proceedings, etc, that originated under previous rules).
Schedule 1AA sets out transitional, savings, and related provisions (for amendments made on or after 26 February 2026).
Schedules 1 to 10 set out the forms prescribed by these rules.
Subclauses (1) to (8) are only a guide to the general scheme and effect of these rules.
 Application
5 Application of these rules
All proceedings in the Family Court must be brought and dealt with in accordance with these rules, except to the extent that the family law Act under which they are brought provides for them to be brought or dealt with under some enactment other than these rules.
Parts 1 to 4 and 6 (the general rules) apply to all proceedings in the Family Court.
However, the application of the general rules to any proceedings is modified,—
in the case of proceedings under the Acts referred to in rule 6(1), by any rules in Part 5 that apply to those proceedings; and
in the case of proceedings to which Part 5A applies, by the rules in Part 5A.
Rules in Part 5 that are referred to in a paragraph of rule 6(1) apply only to proceedings brought under the 1 or more family law Acts referred to in that paragraph of rule 6(1).
Subclauses (2) to (4) are subject to Part 1A (e-solution).
In this rule, proceedings in the Family Court includes all proceedings in the District Court under—
section 15 of the Family Court Act 1980; or
section 151 of the Oranga Tamariki Act 1989.
5A Limited application of District Court Rules 2014
A rule in the District Court Rules 2014 does not apply to proceedings in the Family Court unless that rule is specifically applied by these rules.
6 Special rules for proceedings under certain family law Acts
As well as rule 239 (which gives an overview of the Part), Part 5 contains the following rules:
rules 240 to 253 (Adoption Act 1955):
rules 254 to 273 (Child Support Act 1991):
rules 274 to 303 (Oranga Tamariki Act 1989):
rules 304 to 329 (Family Violence Act 2018):
rules 333 to 376 (Family Proceedings Act 1980 and subpart 4 of Part 2 of the Care of Children Act 2004):
rules 377 to 387 (Family Protection Act 1955 and Law Reform (Testamentary Promises) Act 1949):
rules 388 to 404 (Property (Relationships) Act 1976):
rules 405 to 416 (Protection of Personal and Property Rights Act 1988).
Part 5A sets out special rules prescribing the procedure in the Family Court that apply to applications made under the Care of Children Act 2004 (other than applications under subpart 4 of Part 2 of that Act (which relates to international child abduction)).
7 No special rules for proceedings under certain family law Acts
Part 5 contains no rules that apply to proceedings to be brought under the following family law Acts:
Births, Deaths, Marriages, and Relationships Registration Act 2021:
Civil Union Act 2004:
Domestic Actions Act 1975:
Marriage Act 1955.
Proceedings under those Acts must, therefore, be brought and dealt with under Parts 1 to 4 and 6, except to the extent that the family law Act under which they are brought provides for them to be brought or dealt with under some enactment other than these rules.
7A Transitional, savings, and related provisions
The transitional, savings, and related provisions set out in Schedule 1AA have effect according to their terms.
 Interpretation
8 Interpretation
In these rules, unless the context otherwise requires,—
address for service, in relation to any party, means—
the address of a place in New Zealand, or an email address, at which, or through which, a document may be left for, or sent or transmitted to, or otherwise served on, that party in accordance with these rules, and either with or without either or both of the following:
a fax number:
the e-solution (if the party is recorded in it as having agreed to use it to receive served documents for the relevant proceedings); or
if the party is a defendant as defined in section 4(1) of the Trans-Tasman Proceedings Act 2010 who is served in Australia under section 13 of that Act with an initiating document for a proceeding, the address of a place in New Zealand or Australia that, under section 18 of that Act, is or is to be treated as the defendant’s address for service for the proceeding, and any of the following by means of which the party agrees to accept service of documents for the relevant proceedings:
an email address or a fax number:
the e-solution (if the party is recorded in it as having agreed to use it to receive served documents for the relevant proceedings)
application means an application (other than an interlocutory application) under a family law Act
approved organisation means an organisation approved under section 71 of the Family Violence Act 2018
authorised approved organisation, in relation to a child as defined in section 8 of the Family Violence Act 2018 and to whom section 62 or 140 of that Act applies, means an approved organisation authorised by section 74 of that Act to take proceedings under that Act on behalf of the child
court—
means the Family Court and includes—
a Family Court Judge; and
a Family Court Associate (subject to the exclusions in rule 10A(2)); and
in any case where the District Court is empowered by section 15 of the Family Court Act 1980 or section 151 of the Oranga Tamariki Act 1989 to hear and determine any proceedings, includes the District Court and a District Court Judge; and
in relation to proceedings,—
means the office of the court in which they were commenced; but
if they have been transferred, or the hearing of them has been transferred, to another office of the court, means that other office of the court; and
is defined in rule 17C for the purposes of Part 1A (e-solution)
DCRs means the District Court Rules 2014
electronic includes electrical, digital, magnetic, optical, electromagnetic, and photonic
electronic address means any personal identifier on any electronic messaging system (except the e-solution) to which a document in electronic form may be directed for the attention of a particular user of that system
Examples
Electronic address
An email address.
A fax number.
A remotely accessible location that—
is in a file directory on a computer hard drive or server (including an Internet-based server); and
is not the e-solution.
emergency means a disruption that arises from—
a state of emergency as defined in section 5 of the Emergency Management Act 2026:
an epidemic notice given under section 5(1) of the Epidemic Preparedness Act 2006
e-solution means the electronic case-management solution (if any) provided, by or on behalf of the court,—
for an office of the court; and
for use to bring or deal with proceedings in the court (see rule 5 and Part 1A) that, if that solution were not provided, would be proceedings permitted or required to be brought or dealt with through that office
Family Court Associate means a Family Court Associate appointed under section 7A of the Family Court Act 1980
family law Act means an Act that provides for proceedings to be heard and determined by a court
fee means a court fee prescribed by the Family Courts Fees Regulations 2009
HCRs or High Court Rules means the High Court Rules 2016
incapacitated person means a person who, by reason of physical, intellectual, or mental impairment, whether temporary or permanent, is—
not capable of understanding the issues on which his or her decision would be required as a litigant conducting proceedings; or
unable to give sufficient instructions to issue, defend, or compromise proceedings
interlocutory application—
means an application in proceedings or intended proceedings for an order or a direction relating to a matter of procedure or for some relief ancillary to the orders or declarations sought in the proceedings or intended proceedings; and
includes—
an application for a rehearing; and
an application to review an order made, or a direction given, on an interlocutory application
Judge—
means a Family Court Judge; and
in any case where a District Court is empowered by section 15 of the Family Court Act 1980 or section 151 of the Oranga Tamariki Act 1989 to hear and determine any proceedings, includes the District Court Judge; and
in relation to any proceedings, means the Judge who is presiding in the proceedings
judgment has the meaning given to it in rule 196
jurat has the meaning given to it in rule 158(3)
lawyer has the meaning given to it by section 6 of the Lawyers and Conveyancers Act 2006
lawyer user, of the e-solution, in relation to a requirement to use the e-solution, means a lawyer not exempted from the requirement (see rules 17G to 17K)
litigation guardian—
a person who is appointed under rule 90C, 90D, or 90F to conduct a proceeding; and
has the same meaning as the expression guardian ad litem
manager means a manager of the kind specified in rule 91(1)
Minister means the Minister of the Crown who, under the authority of a warrant or with the authority of the Prime Minister, is responsible for the administration of the Family Court Act 1980
on notice, in relation to an application, means on notice—
to certain persons who will be parties to, or interested in, or likely to be affected by, the proceedings; and
given by certain documents relating to the application (for example, a copy of some of the documents required to be filed to make the application) being served on (that is, in general terms, delivered to) those persons
reasons for judgment has the meaning given to it in rule 196
Registrar, in relation to the court,—
means a Registrar of the court (who may be a Registrar of both the Family Court and the District Court); and
includes a Deputy Registrar (who may be a Deputy Registrar of both the Family Court and the District Court)
Registrar's list means a list—
on which a Registrar, under rule 32(1)﻿(a) or rule 229(1)﻿(b)﻿(ii), enters applications that are not yet ready to set down for a hearing, so that they may be regularly monitored, and managed and progressed towards either resolution without a hearing or readiness for setting down for a hearing; and
on which the court or a Registrar enters applications the hearings of which are postponed or adjourned (for example, under rule 192 or rule 234), so that they may be regularly monitored, and managed and progressed towards either resolution without a hearing or readiness for hearing
Registrar's list date, in relation to a Registrar's list, means the date on which applications entered on that list will next be reviewed by the Registrar
Registrar’s list of applications for dissolution orders means a list on which a Registrar enters applications for an order dissolving a marriage or civil union made under—
section 37 of the Family Proceedings Act 1980 (that is, applications for a dissolution order: irreconcilable breakdown); and
section 39A of the Family Proceedings Act 1980 (that is, applications for a dissolution order: protected person under protection order)
representative means—
a person treated as appointed as a next friend for a minor under rule 90B; or
a person appointed as a litigation guardian for a minor under rule 90C; or
in relation to a child as defined in section 8 of the Family Violence Act 2018 and to whom section 62 or 140 of that Act applies, an authorised approved organisation; or
in relation to a person lacking capacity to whom section 67 of the Family Violence Act 2018 applies, a person appointed under rule 90D as a litigation guardian, or an approved organisation authorised by section 74 of that Act, to take proceedings under that Act on behalf of that person; or
a person appointed under rule 90F as a litigation guardian for an incapacitated person; or
in relation to a person to whom section 69 of the Family Violence Act 2018 applies who is unable to make an application personally by reason of physical incapacity, fear of harm, or other sufficient cause, a litigation guardian appointed under section 69 of that Act, or an approved organisation authorised by section 74 of that Act, to take proceedings under that Act on behalf of that person; or
a person appointed under section 13 of the Harassment Act 1997 as a representative of a person who is unable or unwilling to make an application personally by reason of physical incapacity or fear of harm or other sufficient cause; or
a person appointed as a guardian ad litem for a person under section 10(1)﻿(i) of the Protection of Personal and Property Rights Act 1988; or
a person appointed as an attorney for another person under an enduring power of attorney for the purpose of Part 9 of the Protection of Personal and Property Rights Act 1988 (but only if that enduring power of attorney authorises the attorney to bring or defend proceedings on behalf of the person, and only in a proceeding to which that authority extends)
social worker has the same meaning as in section 2(1) of the Oranga Tamariki Act 1989
user, of the e-solution, means—
the court (as defined in this subclause) or a Registrar; or
a person who may or must use the e-solution to bring or deal with proceedings in the court (see rule 5 and Part 1A)
without notice, in relation to an application under a family law Act or the District Court Rules 2014, includes an application that the Act provides, or that those rules provide, may be made ex parte
working day, in relation to a court, means a day on which the court office is not closed under DCR 2.1 or 2.3 (and DCR 1.22 applies accordingly).
Wherever the term court office is used in these rules, the term court registry or a similar term may be used instead, whether in forms or other documents used for the purposes of these rules or in proceedings to which these rules apply, or as the case requires, for any other legal purpose.
9 Words and expressions defined in Acts
Unless the context otherwise requires, a word or expression used in a rule or form in these rules and defined in an Act must be given the meaning the Act gives it if—
the Act deals with the subject matter of the rule or form; and
the word or expression is not defined in these rules.
10 References to prescribed forms
In these rules, a reference to a numbered form is a reference to that form as set out in Schedules 1 to 10.
For requirements to use forms, see rules 62, 62A, and 62AB, and the special rules in Parts 5 and 5A.
10AA Legal requirements relating to writing may be met by electronic means
This rule applies to a legal requirement—
in a provision of these rules; and
that is a legal requirement relating to writing.
The requirement may be met by electronic means.
However, subclause (2) does not apply if—
these rules provide otherwise; or
the context otherwise requires.
In this rule,—
legal requirement includes a provision that provides consequences that depend on whether or not the provision is complied with
legal requirement relating to writing means—
a legal requirement that information be in writing; or
a legal requirement that information be recorded in writing; or
a legal requirement to give or provide information in writing.
 Family Court Associates
10A Jurisdiction and powers of Family Court Associates
In addition to the jurisdiction and powers conferred on Family Court Associates by the Family Court Act 1980 or any other enactment, Family Court Associates have the jurisdiction and powers specified in these rules.
However, nothing in these rules confers, or is to be taken to confer, any jurisdiction or powers of the court or a Judge on Family Court Associates—
in relation to proceedings under—
the Intellectual Disability (Compulsory Care and Rehabilitation) Act 2003:
the Mental Health (Compulsory Assessment and Treatment) Act 1992:
the Oranga Tamariki Act 1989:
the Substance Addiction (Compulsory Assessment and Treatment) Act 2017:
to take any step in a proceeding under the Care of Children Act 2004 that is classified as a complex case (see rule 416UA):
to hear and determine an interlocutory application that is—
for an order under section 12B(2) of the Family Court Act 1980:
made without notice in reliance on rule 220(2)﻿(a):
made without notice under rule 220A:
an application for a rehearing:
to hear and determine a substantive application (whether made on notice or without notice) unless authorised by the Act under which the application is made:
to commit a person to a prison or to enforce an order by committal:
to review any decision of a Judge.
 Registrars' duties and powers
11 Requirements imposed on Registrars by rules
A requirement imposed on a Registrar by these rules to do a thing is a requirement to take all reasonably practicable steps to ensure that the thing is done.
12 Powers of Registrars under rules
A Registrar has all the powers of a Judge to do any of the following if, and to the extent that, these rules authorise a Registrar to do any of the following:
hear and determine any proceedings:
make any direction or order.
Nothing in this rule—
authorises a Registrar to commit a person to a prison or to enforce an order by committal:
limits any right of review by a Family Court Associate or Judge of a decision of a Registrar.
An order made by a Registrar under these rules has the same effect, and is enforceable in the same manner, as if it were an order of a Judge.
An order made by a Family Court Associate or Judge may be signed by a Registrar in his or her own name and description.
 General matters
13 Practices must be consistent
A practice that is not consistent with these rules or a family law Act must not be followed in the court.
Subclause (1) overrides rules 14 to 16.
14 Directions in case of doubt
A person in doubt about any matter of procedure under these rules may make an interlocutory application without notice to the court for directions, and the court may make a decision and give directions on that matter.
If there is a doubt about the application of a rule to any proceedings, the court may make a decision and give directions—
on an interlocutory application without notice made for the purpose by any person; or
on its own initiative.
A step taken in accordance with directions under this rule is in accordance with these rules.
This rule is subject to rule 13(1).
15 Matters not expressly provided for in rules
The court must deal with any matter not provided for by any enactment (including any of these rules)—
under provisions of these rules dealing with similar matters if that can be done; or
in a way decided by the court, in the light of the purpose of these rules, if the court considers the matter cannot be dealt with under provisions of these rules dealing with similar matters.
This rule is subject to rule 13(1).
16 Directions to regulate court’s business
A Family Court Associate or Judge presiding over the court may, at any time, give any directions that they think proper for regulating the court’s business.
This rule is subject to rule 13(1).
17 Failure to comply with rules
If these rules have not been complied with in beginning or purporting to begin any proceedings, or at any stage in the course of or in connection with any proceedings, that failure to comply—
must be treated as an irregularity; and
does not nullify—
the proceedings; or
any step taken in the proceedings; or
any document, judgment, or order in the proceedings.
If these rules have not been fully complied with at some stage of any proceedings,—
the Judge may set the proceedings aside entirely or take any or all of the following steps:
set the proceedings aside in part:
set aside, wholly or in part, any step in them:
set aside, wholly or in part, any document, judgment, or order in them:
allow the proceedings to be amended; and
whether or not he or she acts under paragraph (a), the Judge may make any other order (for example, as to costs, or adjourning the proceedings) he or she thinks appropriate.
The Judge must not set the proceedings aside entirely just because they were not commenced by the kind of application required by these rules.
The Judge must not act under subclause (2)﻿(a) on an interlocutory application made by a party unless—
the application has been made within a reasonable time of the non-compliance concerned; and
the party has not taken any fresh step after becoming aware of the non-compliance.
Subclause (4) does not prevent the Judge from making an order on his or her own initiative (whether or not a party has made an interlocutory application for the purpose).
 Part 1A — E-solution
 Overview of Part
17A Overview of this Part
This Part contains provisions about how the e-solution may or must be used to bring or deal with proceedings in the Family Court (see rule 5).
This rule is only a guide to the general scheme and effect of this Part.
 Using e-solution to bring or deal with proceedings
17B Principles
A user may provide information to another user by making the information available through the e-solution to the other user.
A user may provide a document to another user by making available through the e-solution to the other user the information that the document is required to contain.
A user may authenticate information by e-authenticating it through the e-solution.
A user may use the e-solution to access e-solution documents—
under rules 426 to 430 (access under entitlement in rule, or under permission order); and
under rules 430A to 430K (access under default arrangement, or under access direction or order).
The principles in this rule are subject to rules 17C to 17K.
17C Definitions
In this Part, unless the context otherwise requires,—
authenticate, in relation to information, means to authenticate the information as permitted or required by legislation outside this Part, and in any 1 or more of the following ways:
establishing the user’s identity (for example, by relying on the user’s identity, and status, as a user of the e-solution):
approving, authorising, endorsing, or verifying the information:
signing or sealing, or signing and sealing, the information:
attesting or certifying the information as a witness to another person’s authentication of it:
authenticating or approving, or authenticating and approving, the information in any other way or ways
court—
has the meaning given in rule 8(1) (and therefore includes—
a Family Court Judge; and
a Family Court Associate (subject to the exclusions in rule 10A(2))); but
includes a Registrar
electronic has the meaning given in rule 8(1)
e-solution has the meaning given in rule 8(1)
information includes information in electronic form
Example
Information in document
The information may be information contained in a document (for example, an electronic copy of a paper, or other non-electronic, document), regardless of how legislation outside this Part refers to the document (for example, as any affidavit, application, authority, declaration, direction, document, form, judgment, memorandum, notice, order, pleading, or report).
lawyer user has the meaning given in rule 8(1)
provide, in relation to information, means to provide it as permitted or required by a rule outside this Part or by a family law Act, for example, by—
advising the court, or a person, of the information:
giving the court, or a person, notice of the information:
presenting it, as a document (for example, to make, or in connection with, an application or interlocutory application), for filing in a court office or in the court:
informing the court, or a person, of the information:
notifying the court, or a person, of the information:
sending the information (whether or not as a document) to the court or to a person:
serving the information, as a document, on a person
user has the meaning given in rule 8(1).
17D Providing information to another user
A user may provide information to another user by making the information available through the e-solution to the other user (see rule 17B(1)) if—
the user providing the information is a user (other than the court), and the other user receiving the information is the court; or
the user providing the information is the court, and the other user receiving the information is—
a lawyer user; or
a user recorded in the e-solution as having agreed to use the e-solution to receive court-provided information for the proceedings.
A user may provide information to another user by making the information available through the e-solution to the other user (see rule 17B(1)) if the user providing the information is a user (other than the court), the information is a document that may, or must, be served on the other user, and the other user receiving the information—
is a lawyer user; or
is recorded in the e-solution as having agreed to use the e-solution to receive documents served party-to-party for the proceedings.
A user providing information to another user by making the information available through the e-solution to the other user (see rule 17B(1)), under subclause (1) or (2) of this rule,—
does not limit requirements in these rules that a document specify a place (for example, a person’s last known, usual, or other place of residence or business), the proper office of the court, or the relevant place of the court (for example, for conferences or hearings, or for complying with a summons); and
is subject to any legislation that requires a specified document (or a copy of it) to be—
issued physically to a person; or
served on a person by personal service on that person; or
served on a person by delivery or post to an address that is the address of a place; or
personally delivered, or personally given, to a person; and
is subject to rule 17G (lawyers must use e-solution unless exempted); and
is subject to rule 75(1)﻿(b) (to file document, person seeking to file it must pay any applicable fee not waived or postponed by Registrar); and
is subject to rule 75(2) (document is filed when it is accepted for filing by Registrar); but
otherwise overrides rules outside this Part (except rule 5(1)).
17E E-authenticating information
A user authenticating information by e-authenticating it through the e-solution (see rule 17B(3))—
does not affect or limit rule 206A (authentication of electronic documents transmitted by Family Court Associates or Judges); and
does not affect or limit rule 206B (electronic transmission of judgment by Registrar); and
overrides other rules outside this Part (except rule 5(1)); and
does not affect or limit information that is authenticated as permitted or required by legislation outside this Part, and that is not e-authenticated through the e-solution, from being—
provided under rule 17B(1); or
used, in or from the e-solution, in proceedings in the court; and
does not affect or limit the validity of information that is e-authenticated through the e-solution and provided to a person—
as permitted or required by legislation outside this Part; and
otherwise than through the e-solution.
17F When information provided treated as served or received
Information provided to a user by making the information available through the e-solution to the user is treated as having been served on or received by the user at the time stated in rule 76A, 114(4), or 119(3A).
This rule overrides rules outside this Part (except rule 5(1)).
 Requirements for lawyers to use e-solution
17G Lawyers must use e-solution unless exempted
A lawyer must use the e-solution in the following ways to bring or deal with proceedings in the Family Court in which the lawyer is or will be acting:
to provide information to the court under rule 17D(1)﻿(a):
to receive information provided by the court under rule 17D(1)﻿(b):
to receive information provided by another user (that is a document that may or must be served on the lawyer) under rule 17D(2)﻿(a).
This rule applies—
regardless of the person for whom, or role in which, the lawyer is or will be acting in the proceedings concerned:
even if an agent or employee is acting for the lawyer:
even if the e-solution was not used (by any person) to make the application that commenced the proceedings concerned.
This rule does not prevent a lawyer from communicating with the court, or with a court office, otherwise than through the e-solution—
to facilitate the lawyer’s compliance with this rule; or
for a purpose that is unrelated to compliance with this rule.
This rule overrides rules outside this Part (except rule 5(1)).
However, this rule does not apply—
if the lawyer is providing the court information that is or relates to an oral application, oral submission, or other information that is provided orally to the court in accordance with these rules; or
to the extent that the lawyer is exempted from a requirement to use the e-solution in relation to the proceedings concerned; or
if rule 17K (when e-solution cannot reasonably be used) applies.
17H Court may make order that exempts lawyer from complying
A Judge, Family Court Associate, or Registrar may make an order that exempts a lawyer from complying with all or any requirements in rule 17G.
The order may be made only—
for good reason consistent with the purpose of these rules; and
after having regard to all in-force directions made under rule 17J.
The order may be made—
on the Judge’s, Family Court Associate’s, or Registrar’s own initiative; or
on an interlocutory application that seeks the order and that may be made by the lawyer without using the e-solution.
The order may be made on any terms or conditions the Judge, Family Court Associate, or Registrar thinks appropriate in the circumstances.
17I Review of Registrar’s decision under rule 17H
A Judge or Family Court Associate may review a Registrar’s decision to make, or to decline to make, an order under rule 17H.
The review of the decision may be done—
on the Judge’s or Family Court Associate’s own initiative; or
on an interlocutory application that seeks a review of the decision and that may be made by the lawyer without using the e-solution.
In the review, the Judge or Family Court Associate may confirm, vary, or rescind the Registrar’s decision to make, or to decline to make, an order under rule 17H.
A variation or rescinding under this rule of an order under rule 17H has effect only from a time that is—
specified by the Judge or Family Court Associate; and
when or after the order is varied or rescinded under this rule.
This rule does not limit—
rule 12(2)﻿(b) (rule 12(1) does not limit any right of review by a Family Court Associate or Judge of a decision of a Registrar):
rule 236 (order made by Registrar may be varied or rescinded).
17J Head of bench may make direction that exempts class from complying
The Principal Family Court Judge may make a direction that exempts a class of lawyers from complying with all or any requirements in rule 17G.
The direction may be made only for good reason consistent with—
the purpose of these rules; and
the orderly and expeditious discharge of the business of the court.
The direction may exempt the class from—
using the e-solution to provide any information to, or receive any information from, the court or another user; or
providing specified information (for example, based on file type or file size) through the e-solution to the court or another user.
The direction may be made on any terms or conditions the Principal Family Court Judge thinks appropriate in the circumstances.
The Principal Family Court Judge must ensure that reasonable steps are taken to bring the direction (and any amendment, revocation, or revocation and replacement of it) to the attention of the class of lawyers affected by it.
17K When e-solution cannot reasonably be used
This rule applies if the e-solution—
is required to be used by a user; and
is not operating properly; and
for that reason cannot reasonably be used by the user.
Example
When e-solution cannot reasonably be used
The e-solution—
is not operating properly because it is offline; and
for that reason cannot reasonably be used.
The user may do the following under a rule outside this Part and without using the e-solution:
provide any information to the court (or to another user):
receive any information provided by the court (or by another user).
However, if this rule applies,—
a Judge, Family Court Associate, or Registrar may direct the user how the user must do 1 or both of those things; and
the user must, despite the rest of this rule, comply with any direction given under this rule.
This rule overrides any other rule in this Part that requires the user to use the e-solution.
 Part 2 — General procedure in Family Court
 Overview of Part
18 Overview of this Part
This Part sets out the general procedure in the Family Court in all proceedings other than those to which Part 5A applies.
It should be read with all other Parts of these rules.
The application of this Part to proceedings is modified by any rules in Part 5 that apply to the proceedings (Part 5 contains rules that modify this Part).
Among other things, this Part deals with the following matters:
commencement of proceedings:
what to file:
where applications are to be made:
how to file documents in court:
procedures before, at, and at the end of, any hearing:
rehearings and appeals.
This rule is only a guide to the general scheme and effect of this Part.
 Commencement of proceedings
19 How proceedings are commenced
For the purposes of these rules, proceedings are commenced when a person makes an application to the court for a particular order or declaration under a family law Act.
 What to file
20 Documents to be filed to make application
An application is made by filing the following documents:
the application form for the particular order or declaration sought, which must, if practicable, list the names and addresses of the persons on whom the application is required or intended to be served (see rule 36):
an information sheet that contains particulars about the parties:
any affidavit required or permitted by special rules in Part 5 to be filed with the documents to be filed to make the application (see rule 21):
any other documents required or permitted by a family law Act or these rules to be filed with the documents to be filed to make the application (for examples, see rule 22):
if the applicant is acting in a representative capacity, a statement setting out the capacity in which the applicant is acting, as required by rule 94:
if an order or agreement is to be used in support of the application, a copy of that order or agreement (unless a Registrar directs otherwise).
If the application is on notice, then, together with the documents referred to in subclause (1), there must also be filed the same number of copies of the documents specified in rule 32(3) (except paragraph (c)) as there are persons on whom documents relating to the application are required or intended to be served.
20A Special rules relating to information sheets
For the purpose of rule 20(1)﻿(b),—
an information sheet filed with an application under either of the following Acts must be in an approved form:
the Care of Children Act 2004 (see rule 416F(4)﻿(b)):
the Family Violence Act 2018 (see rule 309(1)﻿(a)):
an information sheet filed with an application under the Oranga Tamariki Act 1989 must be on yellow paper and in form OT 4 (in Schedule 4):
an information sheet filed with an application under the Protection of Personal and Property Rights Act 1988 must be on yellow paper and in form PPPR 14 (in Schedule 9):
an information sheet filed with any other application must be on yellow paper and in form G 7 (in Schedule 1).
21 Special rules relating to affidavits
For the purposes of rule 20(1)﻿(c),—
applicants for an adoption order must file with the documents to be filed to make their application, or file before any hearing of their application, an affidavit that complies with rule 244:
applicants under the Child Support Act 1991 who make certain applications without notice, or who make certain applications on notice, must file with the other documents to be filed to make their application an affidavit in support (see rules 257 and 258):
applicants (and other parties) under the Child Support Act 1991 must, at least 10 working days before the hearing of the application, file an affidavit of financial means and their sources (see rule 266(1)):
applicants for a protection order or for a property order (as defined in rule 304) under the Family Violence Act 2018 must file with the other documents to be filed to make their application an affidavit of the kind referred to in rule 309(1)﻿(b):
applicants under the Family Proceedings Act 1980 who make certain applications without notice must file with the other documents to be filed to make their application an affidavit in support (see rule 336):
applicants for a dissolution order: irreconcilable breakdown, under section 39 of the Family Proceedings Act 1980, may file with the other documents to be filed to make their application an accompanying affidavit (see rule 337):
applicants for a dissolution order: protected person under protection order, under section 39A of the Family Proceedings Act 1980, must file with the other documents to be filed to make their application an accompanying affidavit (see rule 337):
applicants under the Family Protection Act 1955 or the Law Reform (Testamentary Promises) Act 1949 must, if their application is made without notice, file with the other documents to be filed to make their application an affidavit of the kind referred to in rule 383:
applicants under the Property (Relationships) Act 1976 must file with the other documents to be filed to make their application an affidavit that includes the information specified in rule 392 and, if rules 398 and 399 apply to their application, must also file and serve (within the time specified in rule 398(1)) an affidavit of the kind referred to in rule 398(1)﻿(a).
22 Special rules relating to other documents
Part 5 also contains special rules about other documents to be filed to make applications under the following family law Acts or to be filed before the hearing of those applications or by a time a Registrar may direct:
Adoption Act 1955 (see rule 242 – evidence of child's identity, and rule 245 – other documents):
Child Support Act 1991 (see rule 266(2) – further information to be filed and served before hearing):
Oranga Tamariki Act 1989 (see rule 279 – application under section 68 of Act to be accompanied by documents on family group conference, and rule 289 – documents filed on family group conference):
Family Violence Act 2018 (see rule 308 (certificate of lawyer to be included in certain applications without notice) and rule 309 (documents to be filed to make certain applications)):
Family Proceedings Act 1980 (see rule 338 – separation order or separation agreement, rule 339 – marriage or civil union certificate, and rule 344 – affidavit of financial means and their sources):
Protection of Personal and Property Rights Act 1988 (see rule 408(a) – medical or other report in support of application to be filed).
23 Applications generally to be made on notice
An application must be made on notice unless rule 24(1) or (2) applies.
24 Applications that may be made without notice
An application need not be made on notice if the family law Act under which it is made provides, or any other of these rules provide, that the application, or an application of that kind, may be made without notice.
An application need not be made on notice if subclause (1) does not apply and the application, or an application of that kind, is not expressly required to be made on notice by the family law Act under which it is made or by any other of these rules, and the court is satisfied that—
the delay that would be caused by making the application on notice would or might entail,—
in proceedings under the Child Support Act 1991 or the Family Proceedings Act 1980 or subpart 4 of Part 2 of the Care of Children Act 2004, serious injury or undue hardship, or risk to the personal safety of the applicant or any child of the applicant's family, or both; and
in proceedings under the Family Violence Act 2018, a risk of harm or undue hardship to the applicant or any child of the applicant's family, or both; and
in proceedings under the Property (Relationships) Act 1976, irreparable injury; or
the application affects the applicant only, or is in respect of a routine matter, or is about a matter that does not affect the interests of any other person; or
every person in respect of whom the order is sought has either died or cannot be found.
24A If court directs application made under Family Violence Act 2018 without notice proceed and be dealt with as if made on notice
This rule applies if—
an application is made under the Family Violence Act 2018 without notice (see rules 23, 24(1) or (2), 306, and 308); and
the court directs that the application must instead proceed and be dealt with as if it were made on notice.
On or promptly after giving that direction, the court must—
notify the applicant that the court has directed that the application is to proceed and be dealt with as if it were made on notice; and
ask the applicant to confirm, within the period stated in subclause (4), whether they wish to pursue the application.
Unless the applicant has confirmed, within the period in subclause (4), that they wish to pursue the application, the application must be treated as having been discontinued, and the court must not—
proceed to serve the application on any other party:
give notice of the application to any other party.
The period (for responding to the court’s request that the applicant confirm whether they wish to pursue the application) is—
2 working days after the court’s request; or
any longer period the court may allow, during or after that 2-working-day period,—
on its own initiative; or
on an interlocutory application made for the purpose.
This rule applies despite anything to the contrary in these rules.
25 Two or more applications may be made together
Two or more applications may be made together if the applications relate to proceedings under—
the same family law Act; or
the Family Proceedings Act 1980 and the Property (Relationships) Act 1976; or
the Family Protection Act 1955 and the Law Reform (Testamentary Promises) Act 1949 (see rule 385(1)).
However, applications made together under the Oranga Tamariki Act 1989 must not relate to more than 1 child or young person unless the same persons or organisations are to be parties to the proceedings in respect of those children or young persons.
Applications made together may be made in a single combined application form, and—
1 signature by the applicant on the form is sufficient:
1 information sheet is sufficient for all the applications:
1 front page referring to all the applications is sufficient:
each order or declaration sought must be clearly identified in the form by an appropriate heading:
explanatory notes from separate application forms may be combined and amended in the single combined application form if that is necessary to avoid duplication.
Subclause (3) overrides rule 20(1).
26 Documents relating to earlier proceedings
If some or all of the parties to an application under a family law Act (the later application) were parties to an application made earlier to a different court under that Act or under a former Act that corresponds to that Act (the earlier application), the later application must show—
the location of the court in which the earlier application or applications were made (if known to the applicant); and
the file number of the earlier application or, if the applicant does not know the file number, any information that may help a Registrar to find it out.
If the earlier application and the later application were made under the Oranga Tamariki Act 1989 or a former Act that corresponds to that Act, subclause (1) applies only if those applications are made in respect of the same child or young person.
If the later application is made under the Family Violence Act 2018, the reference in subclause (1) to the earlier application must be read as if it includes references to applications for an order under the Family Proceedings Act 1980 or under the Guardianship Act 1968 or under the Care of Children Act 2004.
Nothing in this rule limits rule 433 (transfer of documents relating to earlier application).
27 Documents or forms for filing to comply with requirements
Every document or form to be filed must comply with applicable requirements stated or referred to in rules 63 to 74 (shape, etc, of documents to be filed), for example,—
if the document is not a document referred to in rule 72(4), it must have a front page stating an address for service; and
if the document is the first document filed by, or on behalf of, a party, it must have on that front page the memorandum required by rule 82 (memorandum to be on front page with first document filed).
 Where applications to be made
28 Applications to be filed in proper office of the court
Unless an Act or another of these rules requires otherwise, every application must be filed—
in the office of the court nearest to where the applicant (or any one of the applicants) resides; or
in the office of the court nearest to where the person who may oppose, or be interested in, or be likely to be affected by, the application (or, if there are 2 or more persons of that kind, any one of those persons) resides; or
in the office of the court that is specified in a written consent that is signed by each person who may oppose, or be interested in, or be likely to be affected by, the application.
Subclause (1) does not apply to the following appeals and applications (which must each be filed or made in the office of the court nearest the Registrar-General's office):
appeals to the office of the court under section 30 of the Births, Deaths, Marriages, and Relationships Registration Act 2021:
applications to the office of the court under section 85 of that Act.
Part 5 contains special rules about the proper office of the court for the following applications:
applications under the Child Support Act 1991 if the Commissioner is a party (see rule 261); and
applications under the Oranga Tamariki Act 1989 (see rule 277); and
applications under the Family Violence Act 2018 (see rule 312); and
applications under, and certain applications filed with or in relation to applications under, Part 4 of the Family Proceedings Act 1980 (see rule 340); and
applications under the Property (Relationships) Act 1976 (see rule 390); and
applications under the Protection of Personal and Property Rights Act 1988 (see rule 407).
 How to file documents in court
29 How to file documents in court
An application must be filed in accordance with rules 75 and 76.
30 Procedure if applications presented or accepted for filing in wrong office of the court
A Registrar who considers that the documents to be filed to make an application have been presented for filing in the wrong office of the court may decline to accept them for filing.
An applicant who disagrees with the Registrar about whether the office of the court is the proper office of the court in which to make the application may, by an interlocutory application for the purpose, apply for a direction from a Family Court Associate or Judge that the applicant is entitled to make the application in that office of the court.
If an application is filed in the wrong office of the court, a Family Court Associate or Judge may, on their own initiative or an interlocutory application for the purpose, direct that the proceedings—
be transferred to the proper office of the court; or
may continue in the office of the court in which they have been commenced, in which case no later objection may be taken on the grounds that the proceedings are being conducted in the wrong office of the court.
A direction under subclause (3) may be subject to any terms or conditions the Family Court Associate or Judge thinks fit.
31 Procedure if applications presented for filing but incomplete or otherwise not in order
A Registrar who considers that documents to be filed to make an application, and presented for filing, are incomplete, or otherwise not in order, may—
decline to accept them for filing; or
decline to accept them for filing, but explain to the applicant, and ask the applicant to do, what is necessary to put them in order for filing.
An information sheet required to be filed under rule 20(1)﻿(b) that is not on yellow paper is in order and must, when presented for filing, be accepted by a Registrar.
A person who is dissatisfied with a Registrar's decision under subclause (1) may make an interlocutory application without notice to the court to have the decision varied or rescinded.
32 Procedure if application on notice accepted for filing
A Registrar who accepts for filing an application on notice must immediately either—
enter the application on a Registrar's list and assign it a Registrar's list date; or
fix a date and time for the hearing of the application.
A Registrar must then promptly—
inform the applicant and respondent of—
the Registrar's list date assigned under subclause (1)﻿(a); or
the date and time fixed for the hearing of the application under subclause (1)﻿(b); and
complete, for each person required or intended to be served with the application, a copy of any notice to respondent or similar notice (set out in Schedules 2 to 9) indicating that the application has been made and how, in general terms, it may be responded to or defended; and
issue for service on (that is, in general terms, make ready and complete for delivering to) each person of that kind a copy of the documents specified in subclause (3).
The documents referred to in subclause (2)﻿(c) are—
the application form; and
the information sheet; and
any notice to respondent or similar notice completed under subclause (2)﻿(b); and
any affidavits filed with the application; and
any other document required to be issued for service on each person of that kind by a special rule contained in Part 5 (for example, by rule 408(b)).
Subclauses (2) and (3) do not apply where a Registrar accepts for filing an application on notice for a separation order made under section 20 of the Family Proceedings Act 1980 (see instead rules 341 and 342).
A court may, on its own initiative or on an interlocutory application for the purpose, order that a copy of an affidavit issued for service under subclause (2)﻿(c)—
not be served on any or all of the persons required or intended to be served with the application; or
not be served on any or all of those persons for the time being.
An applicant who has made an application on notice or a Registrar may, under rules 77 to 79, amend the application.
33 Procedure if application without notice accepted for filing
A Registrar who accepts for filing an application without notice must, if the application requires an appearance by the applicant,—
fix a date and time for the hearing of the application; and
inform the applicant of the date and time fixed for the hearing.
An applicant who has made an application without notice or a Registrar may, under rules 77 to 79, amend the application.
34 Orders made on applications without notice
If an application without notice is made, and an order is made on the application,—
a Registrar must, if the applicant was not present at the hearing of the application, make a copy of the order available to the applicant without delay:
a copy of the order must, under rule 101 (documents to be served), be served on every person against whom the order is made:
each person against whom the order is made may, at any time, make an interlocutory application to a Judge to have the order varied or rescinded.
 Before any hearing
35 Documents to be served if applications on notice
The documents issued for service under rule 32(2)﻿(c) in relation to an application (the documents) must be served in accordance with rules 101 to 130 on every person required or intended to be served with documents relating to the application.
If all reasonable efforts have been made to serve the documents in a manner required or (as the case requires) a manner permitted by these rules, but the documents have not been served in that manner, in certain circumstances the applicant may, under rule 126, apply for an order for substituted service (that is, an order dispensing with or changing the service required by these rules).
36 Who must be served with applications on notice
If an application on notice is made, a copy of the documents issued for service under rule 32(2)﻿(c) in relation to the application must be served on each person who will be a party to, or interested in, or likely to be affected by, the proceedings unless the court or a Registrar directs otherwise on an interlocutory application for the purpose.
37 Special rules relating to service
However, rule 36 is modified by special rules in Part 5 as to who must or may be served with documents relating to, or who must be given notice of, certain applications under certain family law Acts, including the following Acts:
Adoption Act 1955 (see, for example, rule 253 – directions as to service of application for variation or discharge of adoption order):
Child Support Act 1991 (see, for example, rule 266):
Oranga Tamariki Act 1989 (see, for example, rules 280, 281, and 284):
Family Proceedings Act 1980 (see, for example, rule 342 – restriction on service of application for separation order):
Family Protection Act 1955, Law Reform (Testamentary Promises) Act 1949 (see, for example, rule 382 – order for directions as to service or for representation):
Property (Relationships) Act 1976 (see, for example, rule 394 – who must be given notice of applications under Act):
Protection of Personal and Property Rights Act 1988 (see, for example, rule 411 – who must be served with copy of applications under Act, and rule 412 – service on persons other than parties).
38 Service of applications under certain Acts
If an application is made under one of the following family law Acts, rule 36 is subject to provisions in that Act that affect who must be served with the application:
Births, Deaths, Marriages, and Relationships Registration Act 2021 (see, for example, sections 30(2)﻿(a) and 133(3)﻿(a) of that Act):
Domestic Actions Act 1975 (see, for example, section 10(1) of that Act):
39 Persons served with applications to give address for service
A person who is served under rule 35 with an application, but who does not give an address for service, is not entitled—
to be served with further notice of any step in connection with the proceedings, or of copies of any further documents filed in court in connection with the proceedings; or
to address the court unless the court permits the person to do so either—
on its own initiative; or
on an interlocutory application for the purpose.
The person may give an address for service—
by stating that address in the first document filed by, or on behalf of, the person (if a front page is required by rule 72 to be with the document, in that front page and, if no such front page is required, in or on the document itself); or
by filing in court a notice of that address, and serving a copy of the notice on every other party to the proceedings.
An address for service may be changed in the manner specified in rule 117 (change of address for service).
40 Notice of defence or notice of intention to appear
A person served under rule 35 with an application may, if permitted to do so by the family law Act under which the proceedings are brought or by special rules in Part 5 that apply to them, file in court and serve on every other party to the proceedings—
a notice of defence to the application in form G 12:
a notice of intention to appear and be heard in relation to the application in form G 13.
41 Time within which notice of defence, or notice of intention to appear, to be filed and served
A notice of defence or notice of intention to appear must be filed and served by a person served under rule 35 with an application—
within 21 days (or any other period specified for the purposes of this paragraph by any special rules in Part 5 that apply to the proceedings) after the person receives service of the application and notice to respondent (or similar notice) completed under rule 32(2)﻿(b); or
if the person resides outside New Zealand, unless the court orders otherwise, within—
30 days if the person is served within the Commonwealth of Australia; or
50 days if the person is served elsewhere; or
if the person is the subject of an order for substituted service (see rule 126), within the time that the court or Registrar must fix on an interlocutory application that the applicant must make for the purpose.
42 Non-compliance with rule 41
This rule applies to a person who—
is served with an application; and
is permitted to file and serve a notice of defence or notice of intention to appear; and
fails to file and serve a notice of defence or notice of intention to appear within the time specified in or under rule 41.
If the person appears on the day of the hearing of the application to oppose or support the application, the Judge must decide whether it is in the interests of justice to allow the person to be heard.
In deciding, for the purpose of subclause (2), whether to allow the person to be heard in relation to the application, the Judge must take into account the following matters:
any reason given by the person for failing to comply with rule 41; and
the effect of the person's failure to comply with rule 41 on—
the other parties to the proceeding:
the management of the proceeding.
The Judge may—
allow the person to be heard in relation to the application on such terms as the Judge thinks fit; or
decline to allow the person to be heard.
If the Judge allows the person to be heard in relation to the application, the Judge may—
either—
proceed with the hearing of the application in accordance with any directions that the Judge thinks fit; or
adjourn the hearing of the application and give any directions about the adjourned hearing that the Judge thinks fit; and
make an order against the person for costs properly incurred in consequence of his or her failure to comply with rule 41.
If the Judge declines to allow the person to be heard, the Judge may proceed with the hearing of the application in accordance with rule 55(2) as if the person had not appeared.
43 Appearance under protest to jurisdiction
A respondent who objects to the jurisdiction of the court to hear and determine the proceedings in which the respondent has been served may, within the time specified in or under rule 41 for filing a notice of defence, and instead of doing so, file and serve an appearance stating the respondent's objection and the grounds for it.
The filing and serving of an appearance under subclause (1) is not, and must not be treated as, a submission to the jurisdiction of the court in the proceedings.
A respondent who has filed an appearance under subclause (1) may apply to the court to dismiss the proceedings on the ground that the court has no jurisdiction to hear and determine them.
On hearing an application under subclause (3), a Judge,—
if satisfied that they have no jurisdiction to hear and determine the proceedings, must dismiss them; but
if satisfied that they have jurisdiction to hear and determine the proceedings, must dismiss the application and set aside the appearance.
At any time after an appearance has been filed under subclause (1), the applicant may make an interlocutory application to have the court set aside the appearance.
On hearing an application under subclause (5), a Judge,—
if satisfied that they have jurisdiction to hear and determine the proceedings, must set aside the appearance; but
if satisfied that they have no jurisdiction to hear and determine the proceedings, must dismiss both the application and the proceedings.
The Judge, in exercising their powers under this rule, may do so on any terms and conditions that may be just and, in particular, on setting aside the appearance may—
extend the time within which the respondent may file and serve a notice of defence; and
give any directions that may appear necessary regarding any further steps in the proceedings.
To the extent that an application under this rule relates to service of process effected outside New Zealand under DCR 6.23 or DCR 6.24 (as applied by rule 130(2)﻿(a) or (b)), it must be determined under DCR 6.25 (as applied by rule 130(2)﻿(c)).
But both this rule and DCR 6.25 (as applied by rule 130(2(c)) are subject to section 27(1) of the Trans-Tasman Proceedings Act 2010, which provides that a New Zealand court cannot stay a civil proceeding before it on forum grounds connected with Australia otherwise than in accordance with subpart 2 of Part 2 of that Act.
44 Appearance for ancillary purposes
A respondent who does not oppose the applicant's application, but who desires to be heard on any ancillary matter (including costs) may, without filing a notice of defence, file and serve an appearance specifying those matters, and, once that is done, no matter specified in the appearance may be determined except on notice to that respondent.
45 Appearance reserving rights
Subclause (2) applies to a respondent who does not oppose the applicant's application, but who desires to reserve the respondent's rights in the event—
that any other person may become a party to the proceedings; or
that any person, already a party, may take some steps in the proceedings adverse to the respondent's interests.
The respondent may file and serve an appearance reserving those rights, and, once that is done, the respondent is entitled to be served with all documents relevant to the rights so reserved that may be filed in the proceedings by any person who is or may become a party to them.
A respondent who has filed an appearance under subclause (2) may at any time, by leave of the court, file and serve a notice of defence and any other document, within a time and on any terms and conditions the court specifies on granting the leave.
46 Forms for entering appearance
Form G 22 (set out in Schedule 1) may be used for the purpose of entering an appearance under rule 43.
Form G 23 (set out in Schedule 1) may be used for the purpose of entering an appearance under rule 44.
Form G 24 (set out in Schedule 1) may be used for the purpose of entering an appearance under rule 45.
47 Getting more information or getting admissions
A party to an application may get more information, or get an admission, from another party to the proceedings, or a person who is not a party to the proceedings, by—
filing and serving on the other party interrogatories, under the provisions of the District Court Rules 2014 specified in rule 137(2):
issuing to the other party, under rule 138, a notice to admit facts:
issuing to the other party, under rule 139, a notice requiring the other party to file in court and serve on each person served with the proceedings further particulars:
applying to the court (see rules 140 to 152) for an order for discovery of documents that—
are or have been in the possession or power of the other party or of the person who is not a party; and
relate to a matter in question in the proceedings:
issuing to the other party, under rule 153, a notice to produce documents:
issuing to the other party, under rule 154, a notice to admit documents.
In subclause (1),—
further particulars means further information required to ensure that the court and the party issuing the notice are fully and fairly informed of the matters relied on by the other party
interrogatories means questions in writing that a party to proceedings asks another party to the proceedings about matters in issue between them in the proceedings
notice to admit documents means a notice requiring the other party to admit, for the purpose of the proceedings only, the authenticity of a document specified in the notice or, by another notice served on the party issuing the first notice, to dispute the authenticity of that document
notice to admit facts means a notice requiring the other party to admit, for the purpose of the proceedings only, facts specified in the notice or, unless the court orders otherwise on an interlocutory application for the purpose, to pay the cost of proving those facts
notice to produce documents means a notice that must be treated as an order of the court and that requires the other party, unless the court orders otherwise on an interlocutory application for the purpose, to produce any document or thing specified in the notice for the purpose of evidence at any hearing of the application or before a Judge or other person authorised to take evidence in the proceedings.
48 Evidence at hearing generally to be by affidavit
Evidence given in support of a party’s case at any hearing of an application must be given by affidavit (see section 83(1)﻿(c)﻿(i) of the Evidence Act 2006).
Subclause (1) does not apply if a Judge, on their own initiative or on an interlocutory application for the purpose, directs that some or all of the evidence may be given orally.
In proceedings under the Oranga Tamariki Act 1989, the application of this rule is, as provided in rules 18(3) and 239(2), modified by rule 299 (evidence at hearing of certain proceedings generally to be given orally).
Sections 106BB and 106BC of the Evidence Act 2006 apply to a witness who is to give or is giving family violence evidence in a Family Court proceeding (as those terms are defined in section 4(1) of that Act).
49 Procedure when evidence given by affidavit
The following rules apply in respect of affidavits:
rule 157 (which relates to orders requiring people who refuse to make affidavits as to facts relevant to proceedings to appear and be examined on oath before the court as to those facts):
rules 158 to 168 (which set out requirements affidavits must comply with, and permit the court to excuse non-compliance with some of those requirements):
rule 156 (which prevents affidavits from being used or read in proceedings unless they have been properly taken and filed):
rule 169 (which relates to cross-examination of a deponent who has sworn an affidavit for an opposite party and, by witness summons, compelling the deponent to attend at any hearing in order to be cross-examined).
50 Witness summons
A party to proceedings may ask a Registrar to issue a witness summons in form G 18 for a person if the party wants the person to attend any hearing of the application and—
give oral evidence in accordance with a Judge's direction under rule 48:
produce any document relating to a matter in question in the proceedings in the person's possession or power.
On receiving a request under subclause (1), the Registrar must issue to the party the witness summons and a copy of it.
The witness summons must be served on the witness concerned by personal service at a reasonable time before any date fixed for the hearing of the application.
At the time of the service of the summons, there must be paid or tendered to the witness concerned the sum estimated to be payable to him or her for allowances and travelling expenses, but not fees, under the Witnesses and Interpreters Fees, Allowances, and Expenses Regulations 2023.
Nothing in subclause (4) limits rule 51.
51 Witness entitled to expenses
A witness attending the court on a witness summons or any other person giving evidence in the proceedings is entitled to receive, from the party calling him or her, a sum for fees, allowances, and travelling expenses in accordance with the Witnesses and Interpreters Fees, Allowances, and Expenses Regulations 2023.
The court may, on an interlocutory application for the purpose,—
disallow the whole or part of that sum; or
order that subclause (1) does not apply.
Subclause (1) does not apply to a person called as a witness by the court under section 169 of the Family Violence Act 2018 (including persons to whom that section applies by virtue of section 150(5), 208, or 209 of that Act).
Nothing in subclause (3) limits regulations 8 to 10 of the Family Violence Regulations 2019.
52 Conferences
In proceedings under the Oranga Tamariki Act 1989, the following conferences may be convened:
a mediation conference (see rules 292 to 293):
a judicial conference (see rules 294 to 295B).
In proceedings under the Protection of Personal and Property Rights Act 1988, a pre-hearing conference may be convened (see rules 413 and 414).
In any proceedings, the court may at any time before or during the hearing of an application, either on its own initiative or on an interlocutory application for the purpose by a party, direct a Registrar to arrange for—
a judicial conference to be convened under rule 174; or
a settlement conference to be convened under rule 178.
A direction may be made under subclause (2)—
on such terms as the court thinks fit; and
whether or not a conference referred to in subclause (1) has been convened.
Nothing in this rule limits the court's power, on an application under section 38 of the Property (Relationships) Act 1976, to appoint a Registrar of the court, or another person the court thinks fit, to make an inquiry into the matters of fact in issue between the parties, and to report on them to the court.
 Fixing date and time for hearing of application
52A Family Court Associate or Judge may require Registrar to fix date and time for hearing of application
The court may, at any time on its own initiative, direct a Registrar to fix a date and time for the hearing of an application.
After the court gives a direction under subclause (1), the Registrar must—
make inquiries with the parties to obtain an estimate of the duration of the hearing of the application, if an estimate is required; and
fix a date and time for the hearing of the application; and
give notice of the date and time of the hearing of the application to—
the parties; and
any person (not being a party) who has filed a notice of intention to appear.
52B Parties may require Registrar to fix date and time for hearing of application
This rule applies if—
the time has expired for the filing of—
a notice of defence; or
a notice of intention to appear; and
a request for the convening of any of the following conferences has been disposed of:
a mediation conference:
a settlement conference:
a judicial conference.
A Registrar must fix a date and time for the hearing of an application if there is filed an application for fixture in form G 17 that is signed by, or on behalf of, all parties.
A Registrar may fix a date and time for the hearing of an application even though the application for fixture is not signed by all of the parties if the Registrar is satisfied that the parties who have not signed the application—
have refused to do so; and
have no reasonable reason for that refusal.
A Registrar must give notice of the date and time of the hearing of the application to—
the parties; and
any person (not being a party) who has filed a notice of intention to appear.
52C Parties to give Registrar information affecting duration of hearing
After receiving a notice under rule 52A(2)﻿(c)﻿(i) or 52B(5)﻿(a), the parties must, without delay, give the Registrar all available information affecting the earlier estimate of the duration of the hearing.
52D Only certain steps may be taken after notice of hearing date given
After a Registrar has given notice of the date and time fixed for the hearing of an application, a party may only do the following:
file—
an application for leave under subclause (2):
an application under rule 14 for directions:
an application under rule 78 to amend a document:
file and serve—
a notice under rule 169 requiring a deponent to attend the hearing for cross-examination:
further particulars in compliance with a notice issued under rule 139:
comply with any direction or order of the court.
No other step in the proceeding may be taken by a party except with the leave of the court granted on the application of that party.
 Steps at hearing
53 Court and Chambers
A Judge may adjourn a hearing of an application from court to Chambers (that is, a venue the Judge considers more private than court), and from Chambers to court, in order to do justice between parties to the proceedings and any other persons affected, or likely to be affected, by the proceedings.
A Judge may adjourn a hearing under subclause (1) on his or her own initiative or on an interlocutory application (either on notice or without notice) for the purpose.
54 Ascertaining wishes or views of child or young person
If the court is required, or considers it necessary or desirable, to ascertain the wishes or views of a child or young person at any hearing of any application, the court may—
order that any party to the proceedings, and the lawyers or other persons representing a party or the child or young person, be excluded from the hearing for so long as may be necessary to ascertain those wishes or views; or
direct when and where the Judge will ascertain those wishes or views.
55 Procedure if some or all parties do not appear
If no party appears, the Judge may dismiss the application.
If the applicant appears but no other party appears, the applicant may proceed to establish the grounds on which he or she claims to be entitled to the order or declaration sought.
If the applicant does not appear but another party appears and opposes the application, the Judge may—
adjourn the hearing; or
dismiss the application.
Subclause (3) does not apply to a person to whom rule 42 applies.
56 Judgment following non-appearance may be set aside
A judgment given where a party does not appear at the hearing may be set aside or varied by the Judge on any terms they consider just if it appears to the Judge that there has been, or that there may have been, a miscarriage of justice.
57 Procedure if all parties appear
If all parties appear at the hearing, the applicant must state his or her case and give evidence in support unless the Judge directs another party to do so.
When that party finishes stating his or her case and giving evidence in support, the other parties must state their cases and give evidence in support in a sequence directed by the Judge.
Subclause (2) does not apply to a person to whom rule 42 applies.
When all parties have stated their cases and given evidence in support, the parties may address the court on the application generally in reverse sequence to that in which they stated their cases unless the Judge directs another sequence.
 Steps at end of hearing
58 What Judge may do at end of hearing
At the end of a hearing of an application, the Judge may—
adjourn the proceedings:
stay or dismiss the application:
deliver or reserve judgment on the application:
make 1 or more interim orders or interlocutory orders:
make, or defer until some fixed or indefinite later time the making of, 1 or more orders on the application.
Subclause (1) does not limit or affect any power or duty of the Judge under these rules or any other enactment or rule of law.
Rules 196 to 206 apply in respect of judgments and orders.
 Rehearings
59 Rehearings
Under rules 209 to 213,—
a party to proceedings may apply for a rehearing of all or any part of an application on the grounds that there has been a miscarriage of justice in the proceedings:
a Judge may consider the application for a rehearing and order that the application, or part of it, be reheard:
a rehearing of the application, or part of it, may be conducted.
 Rights of appeal to High Court
60 Rights of appeal under family law Acts
Certain parties to proceedings have, under the family law Act under which the proceedings were brought, rights of appeal to the High Court against an order made, or a refusal to make an order, or any other decision that finally determines or dismisses the proceedings.
 Part 3 — Rules about particular aspects of proceedings
 Overview of Part
61 Overview of this Part
This Part contains rules about particular aspects of proceedings in the Family Court.
It should be read with all other Parts of these rules.
The application of this Part to any proceedings is modified by any rules in Part 5 or Part 5A that apply to the proceedings.
Among other things, this Part contains rules on the following matters:
forms, and the shape, etc, of documents to be filed:
how to file documents in court:
lawyers and representatives:
service of documents:
discovery and inspection of documents:
procedures when evidence is given by affidavit:
conferences:
judgments and orders:
costs:
rehearings.
This rule is only a guide to the general scheme and effect of this Part.
 Forms
62 Forms
The forms to be used in proceedings in the court are those set out in Schedule 1, unless—
a rule in Part 5 requires or permits, in proceedings under a particular family law Act, the use of a particular form set out in Schedules 2 to 9; or
a rule in Part 5A requires or permits, in proceedings to which that Part applies, the use of a particular form set out in Schedule 10; or
the use of an approved form—
is required or permitted by a rule in Part 5A (see rules 62A and 416E); or
is required by rule 305(1)﻿(b) or (c) (see rule 62AB).
Forms prescribed by these rules may be varied as the circumstances of a particular case require.
Strict compliance with forms prescribed by these rules is not required; substantial compliance, or the compliance that the particular circumstances of the case allows, is sufficient.
If these rules do not prescribe a form for a particular purpose, an appropriate form may be devised by the parties or by the court, using as guides the forms prescribed by these rules.
62A Approved forms for use in proceedings to which Part 5A applies
The Secretary for Justice may approve forms that are to be used by applicants and parties in proceedings to which Part 5A applies (which are proceedings under the Care of Children Act 2004, other than proceedings under subpart 4 of Part 2 of that Act).
The following rules apply to a document that is an approved form:
rule 64 (about size of paper):
rule 69 (about fastening and numbering of pages):
rule 70 (about legibility):
rule 71 (about signatures).
Every form approved by the Secretary for Justice under this rule—
must be published on the Ministry of Justice website; and
