a direction that a settlement conference be convened (see rule 416Y), but only if it appears that issues in dispute are likely, without an issues conference, to be able to be settled by way of a settlement conference:
a direction that the application proceed to a hearing (see rules 416ZD to 416ZH):
the Judge may also make any interim orders they think fit.
416U Without notice track
When proceedings are dealt with on the without notice track, the first steps (which occur before the respondent has been served with notice of the application) are as described in rule 416J.
If a Judge directs that the application proceed to a hearing, a directions conference must be convened before the hearing (see rules 416W, 416Z, and 416ZA).
On an application made without notice, if it appears to a Judge that the issues in dispute are urgent,—
the application must proceed on a faster time frame than applies to proceedings on the standard track; and
wherever possible, a directions conference must be convened within 3 weeks after the date on which the Judge directs the application to proceed to a hearing.
In no case may a directions conference be held on the without notice track before the expiry of the period within which a notice of response may be filed.
The only conferences that can be held for proceedings on the without notice track are a directions conference and a case management conference.
Subclause (2) does not apply if there is an emergency and the Judge does not consider it appropriate in the circumstances to hold a directions conference.
416UA Complex case on standard track or without notice track
The court may classify as a complex case any proceedings that are being dealt with on the standard track or without notice track, but only if the court is satisfied that the proceedings require a greater degree of judicial oversight than is ordinarily provided in proceedings under the Act.
The kind of features that indicate that particular proceedings require a greater degree of judicial oversight than is ordinarily provided include the following:
allegations of serious abuse or violence:
that the personalities or behaviour of the parties, or any of the parties, indicate that there may be a serious risk to the physical or psychological safety or well-being of any child involved in the case:
novel or difficult legal, technical, or evidential issues.
If a case is classified as a complex case by a Judge, the Judge must personally (wherever practicable) take over all subsequent steps in the proceedings unless or until the Judge decides that it is no longer a complex case.
If a case is classified as a complex case by a Family Court Associate, a Judge must personally take over all subsequent steps in the proceedings unless or until the Judge decides that it is no longer a complex case.
A case management conference may be convened at any time in connection with any proceedings classified as a complex case.
416V Simple track
Proceedings to be dealt with on the simple track are—
proceedings commenced by an application for a consent order; or
proceedings commenced by an application on notice in which no respondent has filed a notice of response within the time required for the filing of that notice.
When proceedings are dealt with on the simple track, a Family Court Associate or Judge must consider the application on the papers in Chambers.
That consideration must take place,—
if the application was made by consent, within 2 weeks after it was filed (where reasonably practicable); and
in any other case, as soon as practicable.
Following that consideration,—
the Family Court Associate must, if possible, dispose of the proceedings by convening a settlement conference (which may be held by electronic means (see rule 416W)) and making orders by consent:
the Judge must, if possible, dispose of the proceedings by making all necessary final orders.
If it is not possible to dispose of the proceedings at that time,—
the Family Court Associate or Judge may make any directions they consider appropriate, but must make at least 1 of the following directions:
a direction that the parties attend an issues conference (see rule 416X):
a direction that the application proceed to a formal proof hearing (see rule 416ZH):
a direction that any party provide further written information or evidence, in which case, on receipt of the information or evidence, a Family Court Associate or Judge (not necessarily the same Family Court Associate or Judge who originally considered the application) must again consider the matter on the papers and subclause (4) and this subclause then apply; and
a Judge who considers an application on the papers may make any interim orders they think appropriate.
 Conferences generally
416W General rules about conferences
In proceedings under the Act, rules 174 to 176 (about conferences) do not apply except as specifically identified in this Part, and every conference must be 1 of the following kinds:
an issues conference (see rule 416X):
a settlement conference (see rule 416Y):
a directions conference (see rules 416Z and 416ZA):
a pre-hearing conference (see rule 416ZB):
a case management conference (see rule 416ZC).
When the court directs that a conference be held, the court may give the direction on such terms as it thinks fit, and rule 175B(1) and (2) (about a Registrar giving notice of a conference) applies as if the conference were a judicial conference.
Parties must attend a conference if directed to do so by the court and parties’ lawyers may also attend.
Every conference under this Part, other than a case management conference, must be presided over by a Family Court Associate or Judge.
A case management conference may only be presided over by a Judge.
A Judge presiding over a conference may do any of the relevant things listed in rule 175D(2) at any conference under this Part, other than at a settlement conference (except as provided in rule 416Y(5)).
A Family Court Associate presiding over an issues conference, a directions conference, or a pre-hearing conference may make any of the orders and directions that they make under rule 175D(2).
A conference may be held by way of teleconference, video conference, or any other electronic means, as directed by the court or a Registrar.
As soon as practicable after every conference, a Registrar must provide the parties with a record of all orders made and directions given by the presiding officer at the conference.
Rule 176 (about non-compliance with orders made and directions given at a conference) applies to every conference held under this Part as if it were a judicial conference.
 Specific kinds of conferences
416X Issues conference
The purpose of an issues conference is to enable the presiding Family Court Associate or Judge, having seen and spoken with the parties to an application, to give directions in accordance with subclauses (2) and (3) (as applicable) and make any other directions they think fit.
At the end of an issues conference held for proceedings on the simple track, the presiding Family Court Associate or Judge must—
direct that the application proceed to a formal proof hearing; or
direct that the matter be dealt with on the standard track.
At the end of an issues conference held for proceedings on the standard track, unless a Family Court Associate or Judge has directed the parties to attend FDR, the presiding Family Court Associate or Judge must—
identify the issues in dispute; and
direct—
that a settlement conference be convened (which may be convened immediately following the issues conference); or
that the application proceed to a hearing.
A Judge presiding over an issues conference may also make any orders they think appropriate.
416Y Settlement conference
Under section 46Q of the Act, the purpose of a settlement conference is to enable a Family Court Associate or Judge presiding over the conference (the presiding officer) to—
ascertain whether any or all of the issues in dispute between the parties can be settled; and
settle those issues.
Rules 178 to 180 do not apply to a settlement conference under this Part, except that—
rule 178(3) and (4) applies; and
rule 179(3) applies.
The presiding officer may, on request by a party, permit any person to attend a settlement conference as a support person for that party, and the presiding officer must agree to such a request unless the presiding officer considers there is a good reason why the named support person should not be permitted to be present.
At the conclusion of a settlement conference the presiding officer may make any directions that they think fit and must do 1 or more of the following:
make a consent order (under section 46Q(3) of the Act) settling some or all of the issues in dispute:
direct that the application proceed to a hearing, but only if—
the presiding officer is satisfied that all the issues in dispute between the parties cannot be settled at the settlement conference; and
the presiding officer has indicated to the persons attending the conference that the presiding officer has formed that view:
if section 46F of the Act applies, direct the parties to attend FDR.
If the presiding officer directs, under subclause (4)﻿(b), that the application proceed to a hearing,—
the presiding officer may do any of the relevant things referred to in rule 175D(2); and
the presiding officer may do anything referred to in rule 416Z(2); and
if the presiding officer directs also that a directions conference be held, the directions conference must be convened, if reasonably practicable, within 4 weeks after the settlement conference.
A Judge who presides over a settlement conference may also preside over the hearing of the substantive application, whether or not the parties consent.
416Z Directions conference
The purpose of a directions conference is to enable a Family Court Associate or Judge presiding over the conference (the presiding officer) to make the orders and give the directions that are necessary to ensure that the hearing takes place as early as possible and will enable the determination of the issues in dispute between the parties.
At the conclusion of a directions conference, unless subclause (3) applies, the presiding officer—
must—
settle the issues to be determined at the hearing; and
identify any witnesses who are required for cross-examination; and
approve a timetable for filing and serving any further evidence; and
may require the applicant to prepare and file an agreed bundle of documents (which must be paginated) and direct that the costs of preparing that bundle be shared equally between the parties.
If no respondent (being a person who has been served with the application and required to attend the conference) has filed a notice of response within the required time, and the presiding officer is a Judge,—
the Judge may treat the conference as a hearing and may determine the application at that time; and
if a respondent attends the conference, rule 42 (which sets out when a respondent may be heard) applies as if the conference were a hearing.
If it appears that some or all of the issues in dispute can be determined by way of a submissions-only hearing, the presiding officer must direct that a submissions-only hearing be held (see rule 416ZG).
If the proceedings are on the without notice track, the period between the directions conference and any subsequent hearing must, where reasonably practicable, be not more than 3 weeks.
416ZA Memoranda filed in advance of directions conference
Not later than 5 working days before the date of a directions conference (or within any other period specified by a Registrar),—
the parties must file a joint memorandum, in the approved form, that addresses the matters in subclause (2); or
if a joint memorandum cannot be agreed, each party must file, and serve on the other parties, a separate memorandum, in the approved form, that addresses the matters in subclause (2).
The matters referred to in subclause (1) are as follows:
the issues (if any) that have been settled between the parties:
the issues in dispute to be determined at the hearing:
a chronology of relevant events:
a list of affidavits filed by the party or parties and that the party or parties intend to rely on:
whether the party or parties wish the court to request, under section 133 of the Act, the preparation of a cultural, medical, psychiatric, or psychological report:
whether the party or parties wish to obtain the approval of the court under section 133(10) of the Act for a second opinion on a report requested by the court under that section:
the evidence proposed to be tendered at the hearing, including the identification and availability of any witness:
a proposed timetable for filing and serving evidence:
which (if any) orders made or directions given in the proceedings—
have been complied with; or
have not been complied with, and the reasons for non-compliance.
416ZB Pre-hearing conference
At the end of a directions conference, the presiding officer may, if they consider it necessary, direct that a pre-hearing conference be convened.
The purpose of a pre-hearing conference is to enable the Family Court Associate or Judge presiding over the conference (the presiding officer) to review a proceeding that is set down for hearing and to make any orders or give any directions that the presiding officer considers necessary to ensure that the proceeding will be ready to be determined at the hearing.
416ZC Case management conference
Case management conferences may only be convened for complex cases, and only for the purpose of allowing closer judicial management of the case.
A case management conference may be convened at any time, either by a Judge on his or her own initiative or on application by a party.
Only a Judge may preside over a case management conference.
 Hearings
416ZD What happens when direction is made that application proceed to hearing
When the court directs that an application proceed to a hearing, the court must at the same time specify what kind of hearing must be held.
416ZE Hearings generally
As soon as practicable after the court directs that an application proceed to a hearing, a Registrar must (unless the court directs otherwise) fix a date and time for the hearing.
When a Registrar fixes the date and time for a hearing, he or she must give notice of the date, time, and place of the hearing to the parties as soon as practicable.
The following rules in Part 2 apply, with any necessary modifications, to all hearings held in proceedings under the Act:
rule 48 (about evidence at hearings being given by affidavit):
rule 49 (which applies certain rules in Part 3 that relate to affidavits):
rules 50 and 51 (about witnesses):
rule 53 (about moving from court to Chambers):
rule 54 (about ascertaining wishes and views of child or young person):
rule 55 (about the procedure if parties do not appear):
rule 56 (about when a judgment can be set aside):
rule 57 (about the procedure if all parties appear):
rule 58 (about what happens at the end of a hearing):
rule 59 (about rehearings):
rule 60 (about appeals to the High Court).
416ZF Defended hearings
Subclause (2) applies to all defended hearings unless—
the hearing is for a complex case and the Judge considers that the times should be varied; or
the Judge considers that the times should be varied for any other good reason consistent with the purpose of these rules.
The total time allowed for a hearing must not exceed the total time calculated in accordance with this subclause and subclause (3):
The Judge must determine an amount of time to be allocated for all remaining aspects of the hearing (including, for example, dealing with non-party witnesses).
416ZG Submissions-only hearings
The court may direct that a hearing be a submissions-only hearing if it is satisfied that some or all of the issues in dispute can be determined by a hearing conducted in accordance with subclause (2).
At a submissions-only hearing,—
the matters must be decided on the basis only of submissions (written or oral) by the parties, any lawyer for the child, and any lawyer appointed to assist the court; and
no witnesses may be called; and
no cross-examination of the parties may be conducted (and therefore nothing in rule 169 applies).
A submissions-only hearing must be heard by a Judge.
If, at the end of a submissions-only hearing, all the matters in dispute have not been determined, the Judge may do any of the relevant things referred to in rule 175D(2) and may also do anything referred to in rule 416Z(2).
416ZH Formal proof hearings
At a formal proof hearing, no witnesses may be called, but the party or parties must prove to the satisfaction of the Judge that making the order sought would be consistent with section 4 of the Act.
 Miscellaneous
416ZI Request to speak on child's cultural background
A party to proceedings under the Act who asks the court (under section 136(1) of the Act) to hear a person speak on a child's cultural background, or on any aspect of it that may be relevant to a matter in issue in the proceedings, must make that request in writing.
416ZJ Costs of contravention of parenting order
A party to a parenting order (party A) who applies under section 71 of the Act for an order requiring another party to the parenting order (party B) to pay the costs incurred by party A because of party B's contravention of the parenting order must include, in the affidavit required to be filed with the application under rule 416F, details of those costs.
 Part 6 — Registry matters and transitional matters
 Overview of Part
417 Overview of this Part
This Part contains rules about Registry matters and transitional matters.
It should be read with all other Parts of these rules.
The application of this Part to proceedings is modified by any rules in Part 5 that apply to the proceedings (Part 5 contains rules that modify this Part).
This Part contains rules on the following matters:
court offices:
sittings on days that are not working days:
records:
access to documents and court files (including e-solution documents through the e-solution) under an entitlement in a rule, or under a permission order:
access to e-solution documents through the e-solution under a default arrangement, or under an access direction or order:
information requested by, or available to, other courts:
transfer of documents relating to an earlier application:
transfer of proceedings to the High Court:
proceedings, etc, that originated under previous rules.
This rule is only a guide to the general scheme and effect of this Part.
 Court offices
418 Court offices and hours
The Family Court has offices.
The offices are the same as the offices of the District Court.
The Registrar of an office of the Family Court must keep the office open during the same hours as the office of the District Court.
All business relating to proceedings in the court must be conducted—
through its offices; or
through the e-solution, in accordance with Part 1A.
 Sittings on days that are not working days
419 Sittings when court office closed
The court may sit on a day that is not a working day if the court decides that any matters need to be dealt with.
Despite subclause (1), the court may sit on a Sunday, New Year’s Day, Good Friday, or Christmas Day only if the court is satisfied that the matters to be dealt with are extremely urgent.
For the purpose of a hearing on a day that is not a working day, or of giving effect to an order made at a hearing on a day that is not a working day, the court may authorise the issue or receipt of any document that complies with these rules.
The court may authorise the service of any document issued or received under subclause (3).
Subclauses (1) and (3) override DCRs 2.1 and 2.3 (see the definition of working day in rule 8 of these rules).
 Fees
420 Fee for proceedings under Adoption Act 1955
421 Fee for proceedings under Family Proceedings Act 1980
422 Fees must be prepaid on filing
423 Fees inclusive of GST
 Records
424 Records
A Registrar must keep a record of—
every application filed in the court; and
every direction or order made as a result of an application.
Those records must be kept in the form directed by the chief executive of the Ministry of Justice.
425 Transfer of adoption records
The Minister of Justice may give written notice to a Registrar requiring the Registrar to transfer adoption records to the Registrar-General appointed under the Births, Deaths, Marriages, and Relationships Registration Act 2021.
A Registrar who receives a notice under subclause (1) must deliver the records to the Registrar-General without delay.
The Registrar-General is responsible for adoption records received by him or her under this rule.
 Access to documents and court files (including e-solution documents through e-solution) under entitlement in rule, or under permission order
426 Interpretation
In this rule and rules 427 to 430K,—
access means—
to do all or any of the following under the supervision of an officer of the court (unless the court waives the requirement that all or any of them be done under that supervision):
search any court file:
inspect any document on a court file:
copy any document, or part or parts of a document or court file:
view any document or court file:
download any document or court file; or
to do all or any of the things specified in paragraph (a)﻿(i) to (v) in respect of an e-solution document—
as a registered user of the e-solution; and
through the e-solution (if, and as, the e-solution allows)
case-specific access order means an order made by the court under rule 430I
class access direction means a direction made by the Principal Family Court Judge under rule 430H
court file means a collection of documents in the custody or control of the court that relate to a proceeding
default arrangement means a default arrangement in rule 430B, 430C, 430D, or 430E
document—
means any written material in the custody or control of the court that relates to a proceeding (including any interlocutory application associated with the proceeding), whether or not kept on a court file; and
includes documentary exhibits, video recordings, records in electronic form (for example, e-solution documents), films, photographs, and images in electronic form; but
excludes—
notes made by or for a Judge, Family Court Associate, or Registrar for his or her personal use; and
any document that discloses the address of an applicant who—
is an applicant for a protection order; and
has advised the court that he or she wishes his or her address to be kept confidential under rule 311; and
any material that relates to the administration of the court
e-solution document means a document that is a record in electronic form in the e-solution
first access period means the period that—
is the first period during which a document or court file in relation to a proceeding may be accessed; and
commences on the date on which the proceedings are commenced; and
ends,—
if there is a sealed judgment or order, 6 years from the date of that sealed judgment or order; or
if there is no sealed judgment or order, 6 years from the date of the Judge's or Family Court Associate’s reasons or minute making the order
registered user, of the e-solution, means a person who is registered to use the e-solution to bring or deal with proceedings in the court
second access period means the period that—
is the second period during which a document or court file in relation to a proceeding may be accessed; and
commences from the end of the first access period; and
ends when the document or file is transferred to Archives New Zealand.
427 Access to documents and court files during first access period
Only the persons specified in subclause (2) are entitled to have access to a document or court file during the first access period.
The persons referred to in subclause (1) are—
a party to the proceedings:
a lawyer acting for a party to the proceedings:
a lawyer appointed to represent a child or young person in the proceedings:
a lawyer appointed to assist the court in the proceedings:
any of the following persons in relation to proceedings under the Oranga Tamariki Act 1989:
a lay advocate appointed to appear in support of a child or young person who is the subject of the proceedings:
a care and protection co-ordinator:
a youth justice co-ordinator:
a person who has been permitted access to a document or court file on an application made under rule 429.
Despite subclause (1), a person referred to in subclause (2)﻿(a) to (c) may not have access to a document or court file if—
to allow the person access to the document or court file would contravene any order or direction of the court; or
a Registrar considers that there is some special reason why the person should not have access to the document or court file.
428 Access to documents and court files during second access period
During the second access period—
a party to a proceeding—
is entitled to have access to an order made in the proceeding by the court or a Registrar; and
may apply to the court under rule 429 for permission to access any other document or the court file; and
any other person may apply to the court under rule 429 for permission to access a document or the court file.
429 Application for permission to access documents or court files
Any person may apply to the court for permission to access a document or court file—
during the first access period; or
during the second access period.
An application under this rule is made by letter to a Registrar and must—
identify the document or court file that the applicant seeks access to; and
state what access is sought to that document or court file by the applicant; and
explain the purpose for which the applicant seeks that access to the document or court file.
On receipt of an application made under subclause (2), a Registrar may,—
if the Registrar considers that the applicant has a genuine and proper interest,—
make an order permitting the applicant access to the document or court file; or
direct that the application be treated as an interlocutory application on notice; or
if the Registrar is in doubt as to whether to make an order or direction under paragraph (a), refer the application to a Family Court Associate or Judge; or
if the Registrar considers that the applicant does not have a genuine and proper interest, decline the application.
When an application is referred to a Family Court Associate or Judge under subclause (3)﻿(b), the Family Court Associate or Judge may,—
if the Family Court Associate or Judge considers that the applicant has a genuine and proper interest,—
make an order permitting the applicant access to the document or court file; or
direct that the application be treated as an interlocutory application on notice; or
if the Family Court Associate or Judge considers that the applicant does not have a genuine and proper interest, decline the application.
When making an order or direction under subclause (3)﻿(a), a Registrar may decide to make that order or direction subject to such terms and conditions as the Registrar thinks fit.
When making an order or direction under subclause (4)﻿(a), a Family Court Associate or Judge may decide to make that order or direction subject to such terms and conditions as the Family Court Associate or Judge thinks fit.
429A Review of Registrars’ decisions under rule 429
A Registrar must refer to a Family Court Associate or Judge a decision that the Registrar has made under rule 429(3) or (5) if a person—
disputes that decision; and
asks the Registrar to refer the decision to a Family Court Associate or Judge.
The Family Court Associate or Judge may confirm, vary, or rescind the Registrar’s decision.
430 Rules 426 to 429A are subject to other provisions
Rules 426 to 429A are subject to—
section 23 of the Adoption Act 1955; and
every other provision of any Act, or any other of these rules (other than rules 430A to 430K), relating to access to a document or court file.
 Access to e-solution documents through e-solution under default arrangement, or under access order
430A Access under default arrangement, or under access order
A registered user may access an e-solution document through the e-solution if that registered user is entitled to do so under—
a default arrangement; or
a class access direction; or
a case-specific access order.
This rule does not limit any access the user has under rules 426 to 430.
430B Default arrangement: lawyer for party
This rule applies to a lawyer who is—
acting for a party to proceedings in the court; and
a registered user.
The lawyer may access through the e-solution an e-solution document that is any of the following for the proceedings:
information filed by or on behalf of the party:
information required to be served on the party:
information provided by the court to the lawyer or to the party.
The default arrangement in this rule is subject to—
rule 430F (personally served information):
rule 430G (when access ends):
a class access direction:
a case-specific access order:
rule 430K (rules 430A to 430J are subject to other provisions).
430C Default arrangement: party represented by lawyer
This rule applies to a party to proceedings in the court who is—
represented by a lawyer acting for the party in the proceedings; and
a registered user.
The party may access through the e-solution an e-solution document that is any of the following for the proceedings:
information filed by or on behalf of the party:
information required to be served on the party, if the party is recorded in the e-solution as having agreed to use it to receive served documents for the proceedings:
information provided by the court to the party.
The default arrangement in this rule is subject to—
rule 430F (personally served information):
rule 430G (when access ends):
a class access direction:
a case-specific access order:
rule 430K (rules 430A to 430J are subject to other provisions).
430D Default arrangement: self-represented party
This rule applies to a party to proceedings in the court who is—
self-represented (that is, not represented by a lawyer acting for the party in the proceedings); and
a registered user.
The party may access through the e-solution an e-solution document that is any of the following for the proceedings:
information filed by or on behalf of the party:
information required to be served on the party:
information provided by the court to the party.
The default arrangement in this rule is subject to—
rule 430F (personally served information):
rule 430G (when access ends):
a class access direction:
a case-specific access order:
rule 430K (rules 430A to 430J are subject to other provisions).
430E Default arrangement: lawyer in proceedings
This rule applies to a lawyer who is—
any of the following in proceedings in the court:
a lawyer appointed to assist the court:
a lawyer appointed to represent a child or young person in the proceedings:
a lawyer representing (whether or not appointed by the court to represent) the person in respect of whom an application is made under the Protection of Personal and Property Rights Act 1988:
a lawyer representing (whether or not appointed by the court to represent) a care recipient, or proposed care recipient, as defined in section 6 of the Intellectual Disability (Compulsory Care and Rehabilitation) Act 2003; and
a registered user.
The lawyer may access through the e-solution an e-solution document that is any of the following for the proceedings:
information they have filed:
information required to be served on them:
information provided by the court to them.
The default arrangement in this rule is subject to—
rule 430F (personally served information):
rule 430G (when access ends):
a class access direction:
a case-specific access order:
rule 430K (rules 430A to 430J are subject to other provisions).
430F How default arrangements apply to personally served information
This rule applies if a default arrangement relates to information required to be personally served on a person.
The person, and a lawyer representing them, may access the information under the arrangement only after the court has received confirmation that the information has been served personally on the person.
For the purposes of this rule, the confirmation received by the court may, but need not, be proof of personal service under rule 127.
430G When access under default arrangements ends
Access under a default arrangement ends 6 months after the proceedings end (for example, because they are finally determined, or they are otherwise finally disposed of), unless that access ends earlier because,—
if the user is a lawyer for a party to the proceedings,—
the party files a notice of change of representation under rule 87 notifying the court that—
the party wishes to change that party’s lawyer; or
the party for whom a lawyer has acted wishes to act in person; or
an order made under rule 88, and declaring that the lawyer is no longer acting for the party, takes effect; or
if the user is a lawyer to whom rule 430E(1)﻿(a)﻿(i), (ii), (iii), or (iv) applies, their appointment in rule 430E(1)﻿(a)﻿(i) or (ii) ceases to have effect, or they cease to be a lawyer representing the person, care recipient, or proposed care recipient, referred to in rule 430E(1)﻿(a)﻿(iii) or (iv); or
of the effect of an order or a direction made under rule 430H or 430I.
430H Class access directions
The Principal Family Court Judge may make a direction doing either or both of the following:
modifying a default arrangement for a class of specified participants:
providing for access to information through the e-solution by a class of specified participants.
In this rule, specified participant, in proceedings in the court, means registered users who are all or any of the following:
a party to the proceedings:
a lawyer acting for a party to the proceedings:
a lawyer to whom rule 430E(1)﻿(a)﻿(i), (ii), (iii), or (iv) applies:
any of the following persons in relation to proceedings under the Oranga Tamariki Act 1989:
a lay advocate appointed to appear in support of a child or young person who is the subject of the proceedings:
a care and protection co-ordinator:
a youth justice co-ordinator:
a representative (as defined in rule 8(1)) in the proceedings.
The direction may be made only for good reason consistent with—
the purpose of these rules; and
the orderly and expeditious discharge of the business of the court.
The direction may, without limitation, provide—
for access through the e-solution (different from, or additional to, access under a default arrangement) for a class of specified participants (for example, for a representative (as defined in rule 8(1))); or
that a class of specified participants may not access information through the e-solution, or that specified types of information will not be provided to that class through it, despite a default arrangement.
The direction may be made on any terms or conditions the Principal Family Court Judge thinks appropriate in the circumstances.
The Principal Family Court Judge must ensure that reasonable steps are taken to bring the direction (and any amendment, revocation, or revocation and replacement of it) to the attention of the specified participants affected by it.
430I Case-specific access orders
A Judge, Family Court Associate, or Registrar may make an order modifying a default arrangement, or an applicable class access direction, or both, for—
a registered user specified in the order; and
particular proceedings specified in the order.
The order may be made only for good reason consistent with—
the purpose of these rules; and
the orderly and expeditious discharge of the business of the court.
The order may be made—
on the Judge’s, Family Court Associate’s, or Registrar’s own initiative; or
on an interlocutory application that seeks the order.
The order may, without limitation, provide—
that the registered user may access through the e-solution information in addition to any information that the user may access under a default arrangement, and under an applicable class access direction, that applies to the registered user; or
that the registered user cannot access through the e-solution all or any information under a default arrangement, and under an applicable class access direction, that applies to the registered user.
The order may be made on any terms or conditions the Judge, Family Court Associate, or Registrar thinks appropriate in the circumstances.
430J Review of Registrar’s decision under rule 430I
A Judge or Family Court Associate may review a Registrar’s decision to make, or to decline to make, an order under rule 430I.
The review of the decision may be done—
on the Judge’s or Family Court Associate’s own initiative; or
on an interlocutory application that seeks a review of the decision.
In the review, the Judge or Family Court Associate may confirm, vary, or rescind the Registrar’s decision to make, or to decline to make, an order under rule 430I.
A variation or rescinding under this rule of an order under rule 430I has effect only from a time that is—
specified by the Judge or Family Court Associate; and
when or after the order is varied or rescinded under this rule.
This rule does not limit—
rule 12(2)﻿(b) (rule 12(1) does not limit any right of review by a Family Court Associate or Judge of a decision of a Registrar):
rule 236 (order made by Registrar may be varied or rescinded).
430K Rules 430A to 430J are subject to other provisions
Rules 430A to 430J are subject to—
section 23 of the Adoption Act 1955; and
every other provision of any Act, or any other of these rules (other than rules 426 to 430), relating to access to a document or court file.
431 Open searching, inspection, and copying of documents or records filed or lodged more than 60 years ago
 Information requested by, or available to, other courts
431A Information requested by District Court for purposes of section 46 of Family Violence Act 2018
In this rule,—
Person A means a person against whom a Police safety order has been issued under section 28 of the Family Violence Act 2018
Person B means the person named in the Police safety order for whose safety the order was issued.
This rule applies when a complaint has been made to the District Court under section 45 of the Family Violence Act 2018 for a direction or an order under section 46 of that Act in respect of person A.
A Registrar of the District Court may, on receipt of a complaint, request a Registrar of the Family Court to confirm whether there is, in the Family Court, an application filed by person B for a protection order against person A currently pending determination.
A Registrar of the Family Court to whom a request under subclause (3) is made must respond to that request without delay.
431B Information requested by sentencing court for purposes of section 123B of Sentencing Act 2002
This rule applies where—
a court convicts an offender of, or under section 106 of the Sentencing Act 2002 discharges an offender without conviction for, a family violence offence; and
the victim of the offence does not object to the court making, for his or her protection, a protection order against the offender under section 123B(2) of the Sentencing Act 2002.
A Registrar of the court may request a Registrar of the Family Court to confirm whether there is currently in force a protection order against the offender made under the Family Violence Act 2018 for the protection of the victim of the offence.
A Registrar of the Family Court to whom a request under subclause (2) is made must respond to that request without delay.
In this rule, family violence offence and victim of the offence have the meanings given to them by section 123A of the Sentencing Act 2002.
431C Information requested for purpose of enforcing cost contribution orders
This rule applies to cost contribution orders, being orders that require the reimbursement to the Crown of a prescribed proportion of certain fees and expenses and that are made under any of the following provisions:
section 135A of the Care of Children Act 2004:
section 162C of the Family Proceedings Act 1980:
section 226C of the Child Support Act 1991.
The chief executive of the Ministry of Justice may request a Registrar of the Family Court, or all Registrars of the Family Court, to provide to the chief executive, in a specified manner, the information required to enable the chief executive to enforce a cost contribution order made against any person.
A Registrar of the Family Court to whom a request under subclause (2) is made must respond to the request by providing the information in the specified manner.
432 Information about family violence proceedings available to criminal court
In this rule,—
civil proceeding means a proceeding in the Family Court under the Family Violence Act 2018
court file means a collection of documents that relate to a civil proceeding and are in the custody or control of a court
criminal proceeding means a proceeding for an offence against any enactment (including the Family Violence Act 2018) that consists of or includes conduct that is family violence
database, in relation to a civil proceeding, means any electronic system in or on which information about the civil proceeding is recorded
family violence has the meaning given to it in section 9 of the Family Violence Act 2018
protection order has the meaning given to it in section 8 of the Family Violence Act 2018.
This rule applies if a defendant in a criminal proceeding—
is a respondent in a civil proceeding in which an application for a protection order is pending; or
has been a respondent in a civil proceeding and—
has currently in force against him or her a protection order; or
has previously had in force against him or her a protection order.
A Registrar of a court that is dealing with a criminal proceeding may obtain information about the civil proceeding referred to in subclause (2) from—
the court file relating to that proceeding:
any database relating to that proceeding.
A Registrar of a court that is dealing with a criminal proceeding and who has obtained information under subclause (3) must make that information available to the court.
432A Information about CoCA proceedings available to criminal court
In this rule,—
CoCA means the Care of Children Act 2004
CoCA information means information about a CoCA proceeding
CoCA proceeding means a proceeding under CoCA
court file means a collection of documents that relate to a CoCA proceeding and are in the custody or control of a court
criminal proceeding means a proceeding for an offence against any enactment (including the Family Violence Act 2018) that consists of or includes conduct that is family violence
database, in relation to a CoCA proceeding, means any electronic system in or on which information about the CoCA proceeding is recorded
event date, for an application, means, if allocated, the date and nature of a hearing or conference that is to be held next in respect of the application
family violence has the meaning given to it in section 9 of the Family Violence Act 2018.
A Registrar of a court dealing with a criminal proceeding may request CoCA information from a Family Court Registrar to be used by the court when considering—
whether to grant bail; and
any conditions of any bail granted (for example, conditions imposed under section 30AAA of the Bail Act 2000).
A Family Court Registrar may, in response to the request, obtain from the court file, or any database, relating to the CoCA proceedings, and disclose to the other Registrar if the defendant in the criminal proceeding is a party to the CoCA proceedings, the following CoCA information:
whether there are any active CoCA proceedings:
whether any orders made under CoCA are in force, and any conditions of those orders (including, for example, details of supervised contact):
the next event date(s) for any applications made under CoCA.
A Registrar of a court dealing with a criminal proceeding and who has obtained CoCA information under subclause (3) must make that information available to the court.
 Transfer of documents relating to earlier application
433 Transfer of documents relating to earlier application
This rule applies if some or all of the parties to an application under a family law Act (the later application) were parties to an application made earlier to a different court under that Act or a former Act that corresponds to that Act (the earlier application).
A party to the later application may make a written request to a Registrar that documents relating to the earlier application be transferred to the court where the later application is filed.
A Registrar must arrange to have documents from the earlier application transferred to the court where the later application is filed if the Registrar—
receives a request under subclause (2); or
is directed to do so by a Family Court Associate or Judge.
A Registrar may, on his or her own initiative, arrange to have documents from the earlier application transferred to the court where the later application is filed.
 Transfer of proceedings to High Court
434 Transfer of proceedings to High Court
Rule 191 applies in relation to the transfer to the High Court of proceedings in the court.
 Proceedings, etc, that originated under previous rules
435 Transitional provision
In this rule, previous rules, in relation to proceedings in the court, means the rules that, immediately before the commencement of these rules, applied to the proceedings.
Except as provided in subclauses (4) and (5), the things specified in subclause (3) enure for the purposes of these rules as if they had originated under the corresponding provisions of these rules, and accordingly must, where necessary, be treated as if they had originated under these rules.
The things referred to in subclause (2) are all appointments, records, accounts, books, seals, certificates, summonses, applications, notices, documents, warrants, judgments, orders, decisions, directions, appeals and generally all acts of authority that originated under any previous rules and are subsisting or in force at the commencement of these rules.
All proceedings in the court commenced before and pending or in progress on the commencement of these rules may be continued, completed, and enforced under these rules, and accordingly these rules, so far as practicable, apply to those proceedings. However, in so far as it is not practicable for any provision of these rules to be applied to any such proceedings, the previous rules, to such extent as may be necessary, continue to apply to those proceedings.
If in any proceedings to which subclause (4) applies a question arises as to the application of any of these rules or of the previous rules, the court or a Registrar may, either on the application of a party to the proceedings or on its or his or her own initiative, determine the question and make any order on the question as it or he or she thinks fit.
 Schedule 1AA — Transitional, savings, and related provisions
 Part 1 — Provision relating to Evidence (Giving Family Violence Evidence in Family Court Proceedings) Amendment Act 2025
1 Proceedings affected by Evidence (Giving Family Violence Evidence in Family Court Proceedings) Amendment Act 2025
Amendments made by a provision of the Evidence (Giving Family Violence Evidence in Family Court Proceedings) Amendment Act 2025 (except for this clause) apply only to proceedings commenced on or after the commencement of that provision.
Proceedings commenced before that commencement, and not finally determined (including any rehearing or appeal) before that commencement, continue as if those amendments had not been enacted.
 Part 2 — Provisions relating to Family Court (E-solution) Amendment Rules 2026
 Interpretation
2 Definitions
In this Part, unless the context otherwise requires, excluded proceedings means proceedings that are—
commenced (see rule 19) by a substantive application—
made before changeover; or
made at or after changeover and—
involving some or all of the parties to an application specified in subparagraph (i) and no other parties; and
made under the same Act as the application specified in subparagraph (i); or
dealing with any interlocutory application made, or other step taken, before, at, or after changeover in proceedings commenced by a substantive application specified in paragraph (a)﻿(i) or (ii); or
related to a criminal case protection order, or criminal case temporary protection order, made before, at, or after changeover, whether they are dealing with a substantive application or interlocutory application made, or other step taken, before, at, or after changeover.
In this Part, unless the context otherwise requires, included proceedings means proceedings that are not excluded proceedings.
In this Part, unless the context otherwise requires,—
amendment rules means the Family Court (E-solution) Amendment Rules 2026
changeover, for proceedings, means the time when—
the amendment rules have come into force; and
the e-solution is provided for the proper office of the court; and
the e-solution enables users to provide information to, and receive information from, that office of the court
commencement means the commencement of the amendment rules
criminal case protection order means an order—
made under section 123B of the Sentencing Act 2002 by a court exercising jurisdiction in a criminal case; and
made before an electronic case-management solution is provided, by or on behalf of the court exercising jurisdiction in a criminal case,—
for the relevant office of the court; and
for use to commence or deal with proceedings to which the Criminal Procedure Act 2011 applies in the court that, if that solution were not provided, would be proceedings permitted or required to be commenced or dealt with through that office; and
sent to, and entered in the records of, the Family Court, under section 123F of the Sentencing Act 2002
criminal case temporary protection order means an order—
issued under sections 46(2)﻿(b) and 47 of the Family Violence Act 2018; and
issued before an electronic case-management solution is provided, by or on behalf of the District Court,—
for the relevant office of the court; and
for use to commence or deal with proceedings to which the Criminal Procedure Act 2011 applies in the court that, if that solution were not provided, would be proceedings permitted or required to be commenced or dealt with through that office; and
sent to, and entered in the records of, the Family Court, under section 53 of the Family Violence Act 2018
interlocutory application has the meaning given in rule 8(1)
proceedings means proceedings in the court
proper office of the court, for proceedings, means the office of the court in which an application must be filed to commence the proceedings (see rule 28)
substantive application means an application (other than an interlocutory application) made under a family law Act (as defined in rule 8(1)).
 General rule
3 General rule: e-solution applies only to included proceedings
Part 1A (e-solution) applies only to included proceedings.
This clause is subject to the exceptions in clauses 4 to 7.
 Exceptions related to excluded proceedings
4 User may use e-solution to prepare, and provide to court, information that relates to excluded proceedings
This clause applies, at any time after commencement, to excluded proceedings if, at that time,—
no direction has been made under clause 5 that the proceedings be moved to the e-solution; and
the e-solution is provided for the proper office of the court, or for an office of the court to which the proceedings are, or a hearing in them is, transferred, by an order made under rule 186 or 187.
A user may present for filing at a court office a document that relates to the proceedings by providing the information that the document is required to contain to the court using the e-solution under rule 17D (see also rules 17B(2) and 76(1)﻿(d), (3), and (4)).
Before, or as part of, providing information to the court using the e-solution under this clause, the user may also do 1 or both of the following:
use the e-solution to prepare the information:
e-authenticate the information under rules 17B(3) and 17E.
A user presenting a document for filing under this clause, whether or not that document is accepted for filing, does not mean that the proceedings have been moved to the e-solution.
5 Direction that excluded proceedings be moved to e-solution
A Judge, Family Court Associate, or Registrar may direct that excluded proceedings be moved to the e-solution.
However, a direction under this clause may be made only—
after the e-solution allows the proceedings to be moved to it; and
if the e-solution is provided for the proper office of the court, or for an office of the court to which the proceedings are, or a hearing in them is, transferred, by an order made under rule 186 or 187; and
by the Principal Family Court Judge, if the direction applies to 1 or more classes of excluded proceedings specified in it; and
by a Judge, Family Court Associate, or Registrar, if the direction applies to only 1 or more particular excluded proceedings specified in it.
A direction under this clause may be made on—
the Judge’s, Family Court Associate’s, or Registrar’s own initiative; or
an interlocutory application made for the purpose.
Once a direction under this clause applies to excluded proceedings, Part 1A (e-solution) applies to—
the proceedings; and
all information and documents—
related to the proceedings; and
filed, or provided, after the direction applies to them.
This clause does not prevent earlier information and documents related to the proceedings from being moved to the e-solution after the e-solution allows the earlier information and documents to be moved to it.
 Exceptions related to included proceedings
6 If included proceedings, or hearing in them, transferred to office of court for which e-solution not provided
This clause applies to included proceedings if—
the e-solution was provided for the proper office of the court when an application was filed to commence the proceedings; and
the proceedings are, or a hearing in them is, transferred, by an order made under rule 186 or 187, to an office of the court for which the e-solution was, at the time of the transfer, not provided.
Once this clause applies to the proceedings,—
Part 1A (e-solution) does not apply to the proceedings; and
Registrar A (as defined in rule 189) must, instead of complying with rule 189, promptly—
provide Registrar B (as so defined) all documents in Registrar A’s custody relating to the transferred proceedings (including by providing copies of any such documents in electronic form); and
record in the court file Registrar A’s compliance with this paragraph; and
Registrar B (as defined in rule 190) must, instead of complying with rule 190, promptly after a transferred hearing is concluded,—
transfer to Registrar A (as so defined) all documents in Registrar B’s custody relating to the proceeding that are documents that Registrar A does not already have (including by providing copies of any such documents in electronic form); and
record in the court file Registrar B’s compliance with this paragraph.
7 Direction that included proceedings to which clause 6 applies be moved to e-solution
A Judge, Family Court Associate, or Registrar may direct that included proceedings to which clause 6 applies be moved to the e-solution.
However, a direction under this clause may be made only—
after the e-solution allows the proceedings to be moved to it; and
if the e-solution is provided for the office of the court to which the proceedings were or the hearing was transferred; and
by the Principal Family Court Judge, if the direction applies to 1 or more classes of included proceedings specified in it; and
by a Judge, Family Court Associate, or Registrar, if the direction applies to only 1 or more particular included proceedings specified in it.
A direction under this clause may be made on—
the Judge’s, Family Court Associate’s, or Registrar’s own initiative; or
an interlocutory application made for the purpose.
Once a direction under this clause applies to the proceedings, Part 1A (e-solution) applies to—
the proceedings; and
all information and documents—
related to the proceedings; and
filed, or provided, after the direction applies to them.
This clause does not prevent earlier information and documents related to the proceedings from being moved to the e-solution after the e-solution allows the earlier information and documents to be moved to it.

Statement of rights and obligations of person served in Australia with subpoena under Evidence Act 2006
(Front page—Form G 2)
Important: This statement is important. Please read the statement and the attached document carefully. If you are in any doubt about this statement or the attached documents, you should get legal advice immediately.
Attached to this statement is a subpoena issued by the Family Court at [place] in New Zealand. A subpoena is a summons to a witness to give evidence or produce documents. The subpoena attached to this statement requires you to [specify whether witness is required to attend the Family Court or some other place to give evidence, give evidence and produce documents and things, or only produce documents].
The subpoena may be served in Australia under section 156 of the Evidence Act 2006 of New Zealand.
This statement sets out your rights and obligations relating to the subpoena.
Your rights
At the time you are served with this subpoena, or at some other reasonable time before the hearing, you are entitled to be paid allowances and travelling expenses, or given vouchers in respect of those allowances and expenses, that cover your reasonable expenses in complying with this subpoena.
You are not required to comply with this subpoena unless those allowances and travelling expenses or vouchers are tendered to you.
You are also entitled to be paid for your reasonable expenses in complying with the subpoena in addition to any payment or vouchers tendered to you. You may apply to the Family Court at [place] in New Zealand for an order specifying that amount.
You may apply to the Family Court at [place] in New Zealand to have the subpoena set aside. If you want to have the subpoena set aside, you should get legal advice as soon as possible.
An application to set the subpoena aside can be made and determined without having to go to New Zealand. You are entitled to have Australian solicitors act for you.
The Family Court at [place] in New Zealand may determine the application without a hearing if neither you nor the party who requested the issue of the subpoena requires a hearing. The court may hold a hearing by audio link or audiovisual link if the court thinks fit. If a party applies to the court for a direction to hear the application by audio link or audiovisual link, the court is required to hear it by audio link or audiovisual link.
Note: See setting subpoena aside for details of the grounds on which a subpoena may be set aside and the procedure that must be followed.
Your obligations
If the subpoena is not set aside, you must comply with it if—
when you were served with the subpoena, or at some reasonable time before the time specified for you to comply, you have been paid or tendered allowances and travelling expenses, or offered vouchers in respect of those allowances and expenses, that are sufficient to cover reasonable expenses incurred in complying with this subpoena; and
a copy of the order of the Judge of the Family Court at [place] in New Zealand granting leave to serve the subpoena was served on you with the subpoena; and
you were served with the subpoena not later than the date specified by the Judge of the Family Court who granted leave to serve the subpoena; and
any other conditions relating to the service of the subpoena have been complied with; and
you are 18 years of age or older.
If the subpoena only requires you to produce documents or things, you may comply with the subpoena by producing the documents or things at any registry of an Australian court that is authorised by the law of the Commonwealth of Australia to receive them, not later than 10 days before the date specified in the subpoena for producing them in the Family Court at [place] in New Zealand. If you produce the documents or things at a registry of an Australian court, you will be required to produce the subpoena and to pay the cost of sending the documents or things to the Family Court. You are entitled to have the costs of producing the documents or things, and of sending them to the Family Court, paid or tendered to you before you are required to comply with the subpoena.
Failure to comply with subpoena
Failure to comply with the subpoena constitutes contempt of the Federal Court of Australia and is punishable unless you establish that the failure to comply should be excused.
Setting subpoena aside
You may apply to the Family Court at [place] in New Zealand to have this subpoena set aside under section 160 of the Evidence Act 2006 of New Zealand. Section 160 provides that the Family Court must set the subpoena aside if—
the subpoena requires the witness to attend at a sitting of a court and the Family Court is satisfied that—
the witness does not have, and cannot by the exercise of reasonable diligence within the time required for compliance obtain, the necessary travel documents; or
the witness is liable to be detained in New Zealand for the purpose of serving a sentence; or
the witness is liable to prosecution for an offence, or is being prosecuted for an offence, in New Zealand; or
the witness is liable to the imposition of a civil penalty in civil proceedings in New Zealand, not being proceedings for a pecuniary penalty under the Commerce Act 1986; or
the witness is subject to a restriction on his or her movements, imposed by law or by order of a court, that would prevent the witness complying with the subpoena.
Section 160 further provides that the Family Court may set a subpoena aside if it is satisfied that—
the evidence of the witness could be obtained satisfactorily without significantly greater expense by other means; or
compliance with the subpoena would cause hardship or serious inconvenience to the witness; or
in the case of a subpoena that requires a witness to produce documents or things, whether or not it also requires the witness to give oral evidence,—
the documents or things should not be taken out of Australia; and
satisfactory evidence of the contents of the documents or evidence of the things can be given by other means.
An application to set the subpoena aside must be filed in the registry of the Family Court at [place] in New Zealand, together with any affidavit setting out the facts on which you rely.
The application and the affidavit may be sent by fax or email. The fax number and email address of the registry of the court are [fax number and email address].
The application must contain an address for service (as defined in rule 8(1)) to which documents relating to the application may be sent, or through which they may be otherwise served.
The Registrar of the Family Court at [place] in New Zealand will arrange for service of the application and any affidavit.
The Family Court at [place] in New Zealand can decide the application without a hearing if neither you (for example, in the application) nor the party who requested the issue of the subpoena (for example, in a document filed in response to the application) states that a hearing is required. If there is to be a hearing, the hearing may, if the court thinks fit, be by audio link or audiovisual link. You may, however, either in your application to set the subpoena aside or within a reasonable time after filing the application, request that the court direct that the hearing be by audio link or audiovisual link. If you make such a request, the court will direct that the hearing be by audio link or audiovisual link.

(Registrar/Deputy Registrar*)
Effect of discontinuance
The effect of the discontinuance is that—
the applicant's application ends against every respondent named in the order and may not be continued against those respondents by any person; and
the court may make no further order on the applicant's application in relation to those respondents except in relation to any outstanding issue in respect of costs.
Setting aside discontinuance
If the applicant's application has ended against you and you do not wish the application to end against you, you may apply to set aside the discontinuance.
An application to set aside the discontinuance must be made within—
21 working days after the date on which the discontinuance order was made; or
such further time as the court allows.
Advice
If you need help, consult a lawyer or contact a Family Court office immediately.
Office hours
The office of the Family Court is open from 8.30 am to 5 pm on Mondays to Fridays inclusive.

Consent to adoption order
(Front page—Form A 1
We (or I), [full name(s)], of [address], [occupation], the parents (or the mother or the father or the guardian) of [full name], a [sex] child, born at [place] on [date], consent to an order being made for the adoption of that child by [full name], of [address], [occupation], *and [full name], [address], [occupation], his (or her) spouse.
*[If required under rule 245(1)﻿(a),] a copy of the entry in the birth record relating to the said child is annexed.]
We (or I) have read the explanation of the effect of an order set out below.
On the making of an adoption order,—
the child is deemed for all purposes and as regards all relationships to become a child of the adoptive parents:
rights of guardianship and existing relationships in respect of the child cease except for the very special purpose of determining forbidden relationships in connection with marriage or civil union and with the crime of incest:
rights in respect of property and succession to property are determined according to the relationships created by the adoption, but property rights acquired before the adoption are not affected:
any paternity order or maintenance order or agreement that provides for maintenance of the child, if made before the adoption order, ceases to have any effect except as to arrears owing and except if the child is adopted by the mother or by the mother and her spouse. However, if the child is adopted by the mother or by the mother and her spouse, the adoption does not prevent the making of any paternity order or maintenance order that could previously have been made or prevent the mother from making an application for a paternity order or a maintenance order:
the domicile of the child is changed to that of the adoptive parents, but the child's race and nationality are not affected:
the child must be treated as a New Zealand citizen by birth if at least one of the adoptive parents is a New Zealand citizen.
I certify that, before [full name(s)] signed the consent set out above, I fully explained to him (or her or them) the effect of the making of an adoption order as set out in the Adoption Act 1955, and that he (or she or they) appeared to fully understand that effect.

(Front page—Form A 1)

On [date], the court made an order in relation to your application to adopt a child. The order was an interim order only, as required by the Adoption Act 1955, and while it remains in force the following conditions apply:
you have the role of providing day-to-day care for the child *on the following terms:
any social worker may, at all reasonable times, visit and enter the residence in which the child is living:
the child must not be taken out of New Zealand without leave of the court:
you must give to a social worker at least 7 days' notice before changing your residence. However, if an emergency makes any immediate change necessary, it will be sufficient if you give notice within 48 hours after leaving your previous residence.
The interim order is not an adoption order. An adoption order cannot be obtained until a further application has been made to the court after an interval of [specify period]. The application to the court for the issue of the adoption order may be made after [date] *if the child has then been continuously in your care for not less than [specify period] since the date on which the interim order was made or since the earlier date (if any) when the placing or receiving or keeping of the child in your home for the purpose of adoption was approved by a social worker.
The application to the court for the adoption order must be made before [specify date]. The interim order will lapse on that date. If you do not apply in time, you may lose the role of providing day-to-day care for the child.

Notice of application to respondent
(General heading—Form G 2)
To
[Full name], the applicant, has filed an application in this court. A copy of the application is attached.
The order (or orders) sought by the applicant are specified in the application.
If you wish to defend the application, you must, within 21 days after the date on which you receive this notice,—
file a notice of defence and an accompanying affidavit in this office of the court; and
serve a copy of the notice of defence and a copy of the accompanying affidavit on the other party (or parties) to the proceedings. Those copies may be delivered to the address for service given by the applicant (or by other parties).
You should note that if you do not file and serve a notice of defence and accompanying affidavit within that time you may not be able to defend the application. On the day of the hearing of the application, should you appear, the court may—
allow you to take part in the hearing of the application only on such terms as the court thinks fit; or
decline to allow you to take part.
You should also note that the court may make an order against you for costs properly incurred as a consequence of your failure to file and serve a notice of defence and accompanying affidavit within time.
If you do not file and serve a notice of defence and accompanying affidavit, the case may proceed without further notice to you.
Address for service
If you do not wish to defend the application but you do wish to know what is happening, you must—
file in this office of the court a notice giving your address for service (as defined in rule 8(1)) at which, or through which, documents may be left for, or sent or transmitted to, or otherwise served on, you; and
serve a copy of the notice on the other party (or parties) to the proceedings. That copy may be delivered to the address for service given by the applicant (or by other parties).
Assistance
A lawyer will prepare a notice of defence for you. If you want a lawyer but think you cannot afford one, contact an office of the Family Court immediately. You may also see a specimen form of the notice of defence at any office of the Family Court.
Liability as a witness
Even if you take no action, the court may summon you as a witness to help it deal with the application.
Copies of orders
You will get a copy of any orders made against you. However, any order will probably be in force from the time it is made. The fact that you have not got a copy of the order will not be an excuse for not obeying it.
Advice
If you need help, consult a lawyer or contact a Family Court office immediately.
Office hours
The office of the Family Court is open from 8.30 am to 5 pm on Mondays to Fridays inclusive.
Notice of application to respondent residing outside New Zealand
(General heading—Form G 2)
To
[Full name], the applicant, has filed an application in this court. A copy of the application is attached.
The order (or orders) sought by the applicant are specified in the application.
The court may make an order for [set out order] if [set out bases for jurisdiction in respect of each order sought, following closely the relevant provisions of the Act].
If you wish to defend the application or be heard on it, you must, either directly or through a lawyer in the place where you are, send authority to a lawyer in New Zealand, by air mail, instructing that lawyer to act for you. If you wish to defend the application, you must, within [specify number] days of receiving this notice,—
file a notice of defence and an accompanying affidavit in this office of the court; and
serve a copy of the notice of defence and a copy of the accompanying affidavit on the other party (or parties) to the proceedings. Those copies may be delivered to the address for service given by the applicant (or other parties).
You should note that if you do not file and serve a notice of defence and accompanying affidavit within that time you may not be able to defend the application. On the day of the hearing of the application, should you appear, the court may—
allow you to take part in the hearing of the application only on such terms as the court thinks fit; or
decline to allow you to take part.
You should also note that the court may make an order against you for costs properly incurred as a consequence of your failure to file and serve a notice of defence and accompanying affidavit within time.
If you do not file and serve a notice of defence and accompanying affidavit, the case may proceed without further notice to you.
If you do not wish to defend the application but you do wish to know what is happening, you must—
file in this office of the court a notice giving your address for service (as defined in rule 8(1)) at which, or through which, documents may be left for, or sent or transmitted to, or otherwise served on, you; and
serve a copy of the notice on the other party (or parties) to the proceedings. That copy may be delivered to the address for service given by the applicant (or other parties).
You will get a copy of any orders made against you. However, any order will probably be in force from the time it is made. The fact that you have not got a copy of the order will not be an excuse for not obeying it.
If you need help, consult a lawyer in the place where you are immediately.
If you intend to employ a lawyer in New Zealand, you should ask about your eligibility for legal aid in this country.
